#10390. Google will let Android & iOS apps directly integrate Quick Share next year

At Android Dev Summit 2026, Google announced a Quick Share SDK that will let Android and iOS apps directly integrate the peer-to-peer file sharing capability.

The Quick Share SDK is coming in the first half of 2027. Developers will be able to integrate file share functionality directly into their apps instead of requiring users to first open the system Share sheet and launch the fullscreen Quick Share experience.

Google will also offer a Quick Share iOS SDK for apps on the iPhone, as seen in the example below.

In terms of the end-user interface, activating Quick Share in third-party apps launches a bottom sheet “where the users will be able to see the files that are being sent as [a] preview.”

A saving of $50 from the $399 list price.

The ASRock Phantom Gaming (PGO27QFV) gaming monitor is a 27-inch QD-OLED screen with a sharp 2560 x 1440 pixel (QHD) display. It offers a reasonable 275 nits (SDR) and 1300 nits (HDR Peak) brightness, a 99% DCI-P3 and 138% sRGB color gamut, and VESA DisplayHDR True Black 400. For high-octane gaming, this monitor delivers an incredible 360Hz refresh rate and, thanks to OLED tech, a 0.03ms gray-to-gray response time.

Pick up a gorgeous QD-OLED gaming monitor with a 27-inch viewable area, a blisteringly fast 360Hz refresh rate, and a 0.03ms response time. The Phantom Gaming PGO27QFV from ASRock also sports an integrated WiFi antenna.

If you want to connect the screen directly to an audio source like headphones or external speakers, there's a 3.5mm headphone output port.

#10387. Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Even though I knew Microsoft’s new Xbox Nano controller was tiny, I still gasped when I unboxed it a few days ago. The compact controller is small enough to slip into a pocket unnoticed, and it has been designed specifically for younger people, or for anyone with smaller hands.

I’m not young, but I do have small hands, and I’ve been surprised at how comfortable the Nano is during gaming sessions. At just over 5 inches long, almost everything on this controller has been shrunk down somehow. The ABXY buttons are ever so slightly smaller than a regular Xbox controller, and the thumbsticks feel like miniature versions. Microsoft hasn’t changed the D-pad, though, or the size of the glowing Xbox button at the center of the controller.

The big difference I noticed with the Nano over a regular Xbox controller was the size of the triggers. While they are fully analog and pressure sensitive, they have more of a click when you bottom them out compared with the thud on a bigger Xbox controller. They’re noticeably smaller, as you’d expect, and the bumper buttons above the triggers feel less clicky than the regular ones.

I thought the Nano controller would feel awkward after a few hours of gaming, but the grips really help with comfort levels — a lot of smaller controllers sacrifice grips to keep the controller thin and lightweight. It does mean the Nano doesn’t sit flush on a table, but at 101 grams (0.22 pounds) it’s still really lightweight.

I can’t speak for those with bigger hands, but I’d expect the Nano controller won’t be well-suited to everyone. The size makes it ideal for young children, though. I tried to get my 1-year-old son to try the Nano, and he responded with a smile, so I guess he likes it.

I haven’t had enough time to get a full idea of battery life yet, but it seems to drain around 10 percent in around 30 to 40 minutes. That suggests a lot less battery life than the 30 hours you’d get on a rechargeable pack for the larger Xbox wireless controller. Thankfully, the Nano charges quickly, and I found I could get from 30 percent to 60 percent charge in less than 10 minutes. You can plug in a USB-C charger to top up the battery and carry on playing while you charge.

The only thing missing on the Nano controller that I would have loved is vibration support. I assume it’s not there because of an impact on battery life, because even the smaller Nintendo Joy-Con controllers have rumble support.

The Xbox Nano controller will be available on November 4th, priced at $49.99.

#10385. The Xbox Nano has a hidden cloud secret

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft’s miniature Xbox controller can connect directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming over Wi-Fi.

Microsoft’s miniature Xbox controller can connect directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming over Wi-Fi.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft is about to launch its new Xbox Nano controller tomorrow, and the tiny device has a little secret. The Nano can connect directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming over Wi-Fi to improve latency. It’s a feature Microsoft hasn’t officially announced yet, but one I’ve been anticipating since the Xbox Nano leaked earlier this year.

The Nano will be able to connect directly to Microsoft’s cloud servers to reduce latency, much like how Google’s Stadia controllers used to work. This new cloud connect feature for the Nano is being privately tested on the new Xbox Cloud Gaming website, and it will require you to pair the controller over Wi-Fi to Microsoft’s Xbox Cloud Gaming servers using the Xbox mobile app.

I’m expecting Microsoft to announce this as a preview for the new Xbox Nano controller soon, before it’s fully supported across Xbox Cloud Gaming on the web and on smart TVs. I first started reporting that Microsoft was planning a new controller with Wi-Fi connectivity to reduce Xbox Cloud Gaming latency earlier this year. I’d expect the upcoming Xbox Elite 3 controller will also have the same functionality.

Microsoft had also planned to introduce a direct-to-cloud feature on a new Xbox controller, codenamed Sebile, as part of an Xbox Series X refresh console in 2024. The refresh console was canceled, and it’s not clear when we’ll eventually see a new Xbox wireless controller with direct cloud functionality.

#10384. Engineer creates retro FPGA Game Boy console that can rip your cartridges to an SD card natively

The project was nearly abandoned until a supportive retro enthusiast financed the equipment needed to bring it to life.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Joining the retro console revival movement is the SGBC, a new console that can play Nintendo Game Boy cartridges natively using an FPGA core. Built by Thomas Sanfourche, a French electronics engineer studying in Taiwan, the SGBC aims to give players a way to preserve and play their games with the full Game Boy experience, including a link port for multiplayer and trading.

The SGBC uses a Field-Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a reprogrammable chip that can be configured to replicate the hardware logic of another chip, in this case, the Game Boy’s hardware. It can replicate not just the CPU, but multiple other components of a Game Boy as well, but Sanfourche has yet to specify which of the original Game Boy’s ICs have been consolidated into the FPGA.

The SGBC doesn’t just replicate the Game Boy as a console; it has plenty of features that retro gamers would appreciate — the most interesting among them being the built-in cartridge ripping function. If you insert your favorite Game Boy cartridge into the SGBC’s slot, you can preserve it by copying the ROM onto the microSD card in the console, digitizing your collection. It also outputs in 1080p 16:9 without stretching, and uses integer scaling to keep the game's pixels aligned with the pixels on your monitor or TV through HDMI. The SGBC also features an SNES controller port and a Game Boy Link port for multiplayer and trading.

Thomas Sanfourche almost ditched the SGBC entirely; in an interview with French retro gaming YouTuber GyuGyu, he said he went to Taiwan to study electronics engineering after discovering his strong interest in electronics. He didn’t know the local language and couldn’t enrol without certification, so he gave himself two years but ended up getting the language certification in six months. In his second year, he chose to enrol in the FPGA course after being intrigued by the name, and eventually chose it as the subject of his final-year project — a Game Boy FPGA core.

Sanfourche said his project was dismissed when he presented it to his professors: “What’s the point of doing this on an FPGA?” he was asked. This discouraged him and stopped him from working on it — until one day he went to a warehouse to buy a PVM monitor and struck up a conversation with a fellow customer who was a retro product seller and very active in the local retro scene. The discussion eventually led to his FPGA core Game Boy project, to which the seller asked, “Why don’t you market it?” The supportive stranger then offered, on the spot, to finance the equipment needed to market it or at least bring the project to life. The financier told Sanfourche he doesn’t want to be a part of the business, but that he wants to resell the product when he releases it, further supporting the project by sharing updates and teasers to local retro enthusiast Facebook groups.

The SGBC is yet to be released, but the project joins other FPGA consoles by the likes of retro gaming modding YouTuber Taki Udon’s SuperStation One, ModRetro’s M64, and Analogue’s SuperNT+ and MegaSG+. Not only are projects like these reviving the platforms, but they are also giving them modern features and comforts, bringing the question “What if these consoles were built today?” to reality.

#10382. Google shuts down a sneaky malware backdoor on millions of Android TV and Google TV boxes

Google has confirmed that an Android TV device maker snuck a backdoor for malware onto certified devices, which is leading to security improvements for the ecosystem as a whole.

In a post today, Google confirmed that it has cut ties with “Videostrong,” a company that was manufacturing certified Android TV boxes. This is due to the company building in a backdoor that it could sell access to, including the distribution of malware. Google has taken legal action to block the domains and servers that “Videostrong” was using to control the XCPE botnet and malware modules and banned the company and its subsidiaries from selling certified Android devices going forward, or selling software to any other certified vendors.

We found this malicious software built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years, supported by dedicated infrastructure and constantly evolving configurations to evade detection and manage their affiliates. We believe this campaign wasn’t an isolated rogue insider or a development oversight. As a result, Google has cut ties with Videostrong and banned them from making devices with preloaded Google apps.

Synthient (via The Hacker News) compiled a list of potentially affected devices, with the focus being on the Mecool brand that has made some well-known Android TV and Google TV boxes in the past. Mecool has been rumored to be shutting down.

Google somewhat admits that this was a failing on its part, as these devices were sold as “certified” Android devices with the implication that they were safe. The company explains that these bad actors “employed obfuscation techniques to exploit a gap in our tests and load malicious code after the device’s first boot.” With that in mind, Google is updating its review process to protect users against this type of behavior, and Google reiterates that “a device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust.”

Google’s shutdown of the domains and servers used to spread malware should leave affected Android TV and Google TV boxes safe to use – the company says that “taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices” – but owners may still want to consider alternate options.

#10380. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

#10379. Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.

For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”

In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.

Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.

Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.

In a new post from Valve published after we first ran this story, the company acknowledged that following a “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months,” it’s “running a little behind right now” and taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews, saying that much of the delay is “having to re-review games.” It has updated its release checklist for developers, including requiring them to run a game through Steam for at least five minutes. Valve also says that further improvements are on the way for the build review process.

Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Update, November 3rd: Added new post from Valve.

Critical global infrastructure under threat

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China is loitering over undersea cables in “gray-zone” warfare against neighboring countries, as well as the U.S. and its allies, as it seeks to dominate the South China Sea. Several incidents, reported by Nikkei Asia, suggest the PRC is actively mapping the seafloor for vulnerabilities and using civilian research vessels to do so.

A serious incident in September, for instance, saw the Philippine Coast Guard respond to a Chinese research vessel close to an undersea cable. The vessel reportedly ignored radio contact while broadcasting a false ID before manoeuvring to evade capture. The Philippines, in response, sent its largest vessel to respond. That vessel was spotted 37 nautical miles away from the Batanes province, officially part of the Philippines but claimed by China. The incident also saw a Chinese coastguard ship intervene, with Beijing complaining of harassment afterward, according to the South China Morning Post.

There have been no confirmed reports of Chinese attacks on undersea cables, but it isn’t the first report of Chinese attempts to map the region’s undersea infrastructure. A report by the Center for Strategic and International Studies, a U.S.-based think tank in Washington, DC, found that Chinese research ships were spending more time loitering near cables than any other nation. Their purpose for doing so is unclear, but the report made clear that the efforts were “disproportionate.”

One thing that the U.S. is certain about, however, is that China has the capability. A U.S. congressional report from 2025 highlighted that China is actively developing techniques to cut cables, pointing out that Chinese research teams had already publicly disclosed the details of electric cutting devices that could sever armored cables as deep as 13,000ft underwater.

Ryan Claffey, a research assistant for the Center for a New American Security, a think tank that specializes in U.S. national security, believes that a coordinated response is required to stop Chinese actions in the region. Writing in the report, Claffey warns that “subsea cables are digital lifelines,” arguing that “the U.S. and its allies must protect them in peacetime to stay connected in crisis.”

The People’s Republic of China has maritime disputes with several countries in the region. It continues to hold a claim over Taiwan, which it views as a breakaway province. It also has disputes with the Philippines, as mentioned earlier, along with Vietnam, Malaysia, and Brunei in the South China Sea, as well as Japan in the East China Sea.

Authoritarian nations like Russia, China and Iran have all stepped up operations against undersea infrastructure in Europe, Asia and the Middle East. Russia, for instance, has used fishing trawlers for nearly five years to loiter over communication cables in Europe. Iran, meanwhile, has threatened to attack undersea cables in the Strait of Hormuz if the U.S. attacks it again, describing them as legitimate targets during war.

#10377. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless is known for helping you convert a wired Android Auto connection into a wireless one, but the company is now launching a new product – an active-cooled Qi2 car charger.

“AAWireless Charge” is a Qi2 25W wireless car charger that costs $50. The charger is compatible with MagSafe, Pixelsnap, and Qi2 in general, and supports up to 25W charging speeds on compatible phones.

“Charge” also includes active cooling support with a built-in fan that the company says is “quiet” when active.

Included in the box you’ll find both an air vent mount as well as an adhesive base. There are also two magnetic ring adapters in the box for “Qi2 Ready” phones that don’t have magnets built-in or a magnetic case available. AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

While AAWireless adapters remain available on Amazon, “AAWireless Charge” is available solely from the company’s website.

#10374. The Meta Quest 2 and Quest Pro are receiving their last OS updates soon. Here’s what you need to know

The best-selling VR headset of all time (and its Pro-level cousin) will no longer receive OS updates after March 2027.

Somehow, the Meta Quest 2 has been around for six whopping years, and Meta is finally bringing support for the headset to a close after March 2027. The far less popular Meta Quest Pro, which uses the same chipset as the Quest 2, will also see OS updates cease at the same time with OS v213. Meta will continue to deliver security updates through the end of 2027 to both headsets, according to an official Meta developers blog post.

Meta says it will not be removing cross-play multiplayer functionality or blocking access to Quest 2 players, so any games that support the headset will continue to work after these dates, so long as they don’t rely on any new OS features introduced after March 2027. Developers have to give players a 6-month notice if support is ending for a game, so you’ll have ample time to decide what to do.

That means the Quest 2 will receive just over 7 years of total security updates, while the Quest Pro gets two years less (since it came out in October 2022). The Meta Quest 2 remains the best-selling VR headset of all time, with around 20 million units sold over the lifetime of the headset. Meta says that Quest 2 owners can still play games throughout this time, although game support will likely dry up quickly after January 2028.

The $599 Meta Quest 3 and $349 Meta Quest 3S, which debuted in 2023 and 2024, respectively, are the follow-ups to the Quest 2. The upcoming Meta VR Glasses, which will retail for $1299, feature a next-generation chipset, a significantly higher-resolution AMOLED display, and substantially lighter weight and smaller size than either Quest 3 model.

Meta VR Glasses are expected to debut early next year, and with them, a new generation of OS development will take place. Since the Quest 2 uses a significantly outdated mobile chip at this point, it makes sense for Meta to cease new feature development for it and focus on newer, more capable hardware.

New power system is already operational in Ulanqab, China, where it will eventually ‘support up to one million AI accelerators.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Envision Energy says that it has completed the world’s first commercial data center deployment of an integrated 800-volt DC power system. It has been testing the Dubhe Controller, supported by its Dubhe Energy Foundation Model since August this year at its Galaxy AI data center in Ulanqab, Inner Mongolia, China. Touted as offering simpler, and more efficient power architecture, Envision’s system will also support higher computing density, says a press release. The Galaxy campus will scale to 3GW capacity according to current plans, and eventually “support up to one million AI accelerators.”

Currently, data centers rely on several power-conversion stages to achieve a usable supply from the grid. Envision’s Dubhe tech converts power directly from 10kV medium-voltage AC to an 800V DC bus to boost efficiency and slashes the size of the power conversion apparatus. It is built around three critical capabilities:

The system includes integrated battery support for fast response to AI workload spikes and for short-term backup power. It will also support higher-density AI racks, for more computing power in the same area, says the Chinese tech firm. Moreover, Envision’s system has been designed to work well with renewable energy sources and storage. Key to the efficiency and performance on offer is the AI-driven power management provided by the Envision Dubhe controller, smartly balancing power supply and computing demand. Other benefits include a more compact footprint and a reduced requirement for copper and other cabling.

By the firm’s own accounts the Dubhe tech system is performing well. Thus, it looks set to be instrumental to the expansion of the compute resources at the Galaxy AI data center in Ulanqab. Furthermore, Envision also intends to roll out this 800V DC tech at its Mission Gobi initiative, targetting 5GW of green AI data center capacity in desert regions by 2030.

#10369. How to Add AI Content Detection to Your Node.js App

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later someone on your team asks it. Maybe it’s a product manager, maybe the head of content, maybe a teacher who’s just marked forty near-identical essays: can we tell when people are pasting in ChatGPT output?

The short answer is no, not with certainty. Anyone who promises you that is selling something.

But you can get a useful signal, and you can show it to the people who actually make the call: editors, teachers, moderators. In this tutorial you’ll build a small Express app that takes some text, sends it to an AI detector API, waits for the result, and highlights the sentences that look machine-written.

I’m using the Quetext AI Detector API here. It scores each sentence, not just the whole document, and you can try it without a subscription. Most detection APIs follow the same submit, poll, fetch pattern, though. If you switch providers later, you’ll rewrite one file.

Put the key in an environment variable. Don’t hard-code it, and don’t let it anywhere near the browser.

You’ll make three calls. Analysis takes a few seconds, so the API hands you a ticket and makes you come back for the result. Keep the Quetext API docs open in another tab while you follow along; they list every endpoint, field and error code.

Every request sends your key in an X-API-Key header. The minimum input is 50 words, which sounds annoying until you realise no detector can say anything useful about a two-line comment.

And remember this number: 10 requests every 5 seconds, per account. Progress checks count. It’ll come back to bite you in the production section if you ignore it.

Create quetext.js. I like keeping everything provider-specific in one file, so the rest of the app has no idea who’s doing the detecting.

Three decisions in there that are worth a sentence each:

The errors keep their HTTP status code. A 402 means you’ve run out of credit and a 429 means you’re polling too fast, and you’ll want to handle those differently from “something broke.”

It polls every 2.5 seconds because Quetext’s docs suggest 2 to 3. Polling faster won’t speed the report up. You’ll just burn through the rate limit.

And there’s a hard timeout. Leave it out and one stuck report can hang a request forever. That’s a miserable bug to track down at 11pm.

Next, server.js. It checks the input, runs the three calls, and gives the frontend a simple response that doesn’t mention Quetext anywhere.

Look at the mapping at the end. generated_prob becomes probability, and ai_score comes back as a string like "82.50", so it gets parsed into a number. It’s a small detail. But it means your frontend never learns which provider’s field names it’s dealing with, and that’s what makes swapping providers a one-file change.

Show someone “64% AI” and they’ll jump to a conclusion. Show them a paragraph with three sentences shaded pink and they’ll actually read it.

So the page shows every sentence, coloured by how likely it is to be AI-generated. Create public/index.html:

Run node server.js and open http://localhost:3000. Try two samples: something you wrote yourself, then a raw chatbot answer on the same topic. Seeing which sentences light up teaches you more about how these detectors work than any diagram I could draw.

A quick security point. Those sentences came from your users, then went through an outside service. Treat them as untrusted. That’s why the code uses textContent and never innerHTML, as the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet recommends.

The demo keeps an HTTP request open while it polls. Fine on your laptop. Not fine with real traffic.

Submit the text, save the report id with a pending status, and respond straight away. A worker (BullMQ, a cron job, a serverless queue, whatever you already use) does the polling and updates the record once it’s done. The UI says “Checking…” until the result shows up.

Here’s how it goes wrong. It’s Monday morning, 20 editors open their review queues and click “Check” at roughly the same time. Each request polls on its own, and within a few seconds you’ve hit 429s across the board, because the 10-per-5-seconds limit covers your whole account, not each user.

Use one worker that loops through every pending report, with a short pause between calls. Problem solved.

You pay per word, so don’t check every blog comment. Article submissions, assignments and paid freelance work usually justify the cost. Cache results against a hash of the text too. Someone who submits the same draft three times shouldn’t cost you three times.

AI detection doesn’t catch copying. Someone can lift a 2019 blog post word for word and it’ll score 0% AI, because a human wrote it. Just not this human. If originality matters to your product, the same API has plagiarism endpoints that work exactly the same way. The API documentation covers both.

Developers skip this part. It’s also the part that ends up in angry support tickets.

In 2023, Liang and colleagues at Stanford ran essays written by non-native English speakers (all human, written for the TOEFL) through seven AI detectors. On average, the detectors labelled more than 61% of them as AI-generated. Simple vocabulary and textbook grammar read as “predictable,” and predictability is exactly what detectors look for.

Picture an international student getting an automatic zero because a script said so. You don’t want to be the person who built that.

Do that and the detector is a fast first pass that points people at what’s worth a closer look. Your humans still make the call.

You send text, the service checks how predictable each sentence would be to a language model, and you get back a probability that a machine wrote it. Most of these APIs are asynchronous. You submit, get a report ID, poll until it’s ready, then fetch the result. The better ones score individual sentences as well as the whole text, which makes building a sensible review screen much easier.

More than you’d think. Quetext’s API won’t accept fewer than 50 words, and in practice scores settle down once you’re past about 300. A product title or a one-line comment just doesn’t give a detector enough to go on. For short content, either group related pieces together or don’t bother checking it.

No. Detectors make mistakes, and research shows they flag non-native English writers far more often than native speakers. Use the score to decide what a human looks at first, not to block anything. Give reviewers the highlighted sentences, give users a way to explain themselves, and log what you decided. Your users get treated fairly and your support team avoids a pile of angry tickets.

Poll at the speed the provider recommends (every 2 to 3 seconds for Quetext), and remember that those polls count against your limit too. Once you’re in production, move checks into a background queue and let one worker poll every pending report, rather than having each user request poll by itself. When you do get a 429, wait a moment and retry.

No. If the browser calls the API directly, your key ends up in the frontend code where anyone can copy it and spend your credit. Keep the key in an environment variable on your server and send requests through your own endpoint, like the one in this tutorial. That also gives you somewhere to check input length, apply per-user limits and cache results before you pay for anything.

#10366. Android gaming is having a renaissance

Good morning! Today I’m mourning the loss of Patrick Stewart by rewatching First Contact, my favorite Trek movie; beginning a marathon listen of at least one great Star Trek podcast; and replaying Elite Force and Armada, both wonderful Trek games released in 2000.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

I’m starting this edition of Inbox a bit later than I’d like, but for a good reason: I got swept up by the subject I was researching. See, I wanted to write about the seeming renaissance of gaming on Android, which has always had clear advantages and disadvantages compared to iOS. On the plus side, its openness bred experimentation and a healthy indie gaming scene, along with a thriving emulation ecosystem born of its ability to sideload apps and gain access to Android’s underlying file system. On the downside, its device and OS version fragmentation has kept some developers from attempting challenging console and PC ports, and led others to abandon their titles to bugs and time.

But thanks to a confluence of factors, as well as form factors, Android gaming is in a very good place right now. Despite Google’s continued ambivalence towards paid and premium games, the platform has received official ports of console hits like Dredge and Sifu, with others like Replaced, Absolum, and Dispatch coming soon. (One could argue things have been good for a while with Stardew Valley, Dead Cells, Balatro, Slay the Spire, and Vampire Crawlers available already, but I digress.)

Most of these games are embracing a try-before-you-buy model to avoid the upfront cost that so many mobile users resist, while others, like Dispatch, are getting a mobile optimization that reorients the experience for a portrait display.

And while traditional emulation has existed since Android was young, recent months have seen considerable improvement in breadth and performance, aided by AI coding tools and a community that wants to play its library of Steam, Windows, and Switch games on phones. This rabbit hole is extensive and varied: WinNative combines work from Winlator Bionic and Pluvia to run Windows games on Android and connect PC storefront libraries. DroidDeck aims for a SteamOS-like experience, putting Steam’s Big Picture interface on supported Android hardware with Windows games running through ARM64 Proton, which debuted recently with the Steam Frame.

It’s via DroidDeck that I whiled away a couple of glorious hours on my Galaxy Z Fold 8 playing Lumines Arise, a game perfectly suited to the pick-up-and-play nature of smartphones (though it’s best with a controller). The latest Qualcomm chips have long outpaced the mobile games they’ve been asked to power, but few knew just how well they’d run demanding PC and console titles, even through compatibility layers like Proton.

Similarly, an Android branch of the increasingly popular nxnative, which compiles Switch and Switch 2 games for iOS and macOS, has been making it easy to play games like Super Mario Bros. Wonder and Metroid Dread at 60fps on many recent Qualcomm-powered devices. There’s even a Switch 2 version in the works for Android.

The big question is whether all of this works well: will people spend more time arguing with the software than playing the games themselves? And on the legal side of the business, will publishers find success selling discounted versions of AAA titles on Android now that the translation tools are better and the device compatibility much broader?

If there’s one thing I’ve learned covering Android for nearly 20 years, it’s that the community always finds a way.

ICYMI: Ben’s Weekender essay is all about how Motorola is filling the gap OnePlus left behind, mostly.

The strangest thing I’ve seen recently is the rumor that Elon Musk wants to launch a Starlink-branded terrestrial wireless carrier in the US. Now we know how he plans to do it: by purchasing Boost Mobile from EchoStar and leveraging its contracts with AT&T and T-Mobile along with new low- and mid-band spectrum Starlink recently acquired. That infrastructure, combined with Starlink’s existing array of LEO satellites, could make for a compelling advantage over the incumbents: namely, that dead spots practically won’t exist anymore.

But as we’ve learned from countless examples over the years, running a low-margin, high-churn postpaid wireless business is not for the faint of heart, and I’m not sure why Musk has decided that now is the right time to do it.

Five years on, is the Pixel 6 series really the disaster that we remember it being? The answer, according to Damien Wilde, is a bit more nuanced.

The Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro were fundamentally flawed pioneers for Google. They were the firm’s most ambitious hardware undertaking to date, and simultaneously one of the most frustrating phone series to live with during their initial launch window and beyond. If the Pixel 5 was a safe play ahead of this big pitch, the Pixel 6 was a risky dive into deep waters. It stumbled badly. Yet without those painful growing pains, the more polished, confident, and reliable Pixel devices we enjoy today simply would not exist.

Finally, networked supermarket freezers in Sweden, Belgium, and Luxembourg were hacked over the weekend, sending temperatures up to a reported 50 degrees Celsius, and all the food inside them straight into the trash.

According to Reuters, “The latest incidents follow an attack in France last month when police said hackers seized control of cooling units at hundreds of supermarkets, causing fires and food losses.”

What we’re seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. As we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carry-in inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. For the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs…Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase.

#10365. Google reveals new Gemini 4 Carbon AI model, narrowly beating Claude Opus 5.5

Tests now push models to reveal their greater weaknesses.

Last week, Google released its latest AI model, Gemini 4 Carbon, topping intelligence rankings charts. The model has landed with a grander splash than most. As Artificial Analysis highlights, Gemini 4 Carbon is now the highest-ranked AI model on its latest Intelligence Index V5 benchmark suite, beating Claude Opus 5.5 by one point. It achieves the number one score across four of the nine evaluations, and does so at a lower cost per task than other high-ranking models, like Claude Opus 5.5. Google is offering access to the model for cyber defenders first through its Fairwind program, with wider availability coming sometime after.

Perhaps more crucially, Gemini 4 Carbon stands apart by topping Artificial Analysis' new AA-Omniscience v 1.1 test that tracks hallucinations and knowledgeable recall of information. As governments and institutions look to maximize value and security from their use of AI, being able to claim the lowest hallucination score could prove a significant selling point.

Then again, even the best scored only 54 on the -100 to +100 scale, suggesting even the best frontier models in 2026 are a far cry from being consistently reliable when it comes to accurately recalling information.

AI model rankings change fast and regularly in 2026, and it's no surprise that the latest release from a frontier lab has pushed the envelope, but Google's Gemini 4 Carbon can be seen as a comeback after languishing with irregular releases for much of the year.

Gemini 4 Carbon (high) scores a 59 on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index V5 benchmark. That puts it a single point ahead of Claude Opus 5.5 (max with fallback), and two points ahead of OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra (max). It's also nine points ahead of Meta's Muse Wonder Full (Max), and 10 points ahead of Claude Fable 5.1 (max with fallback).

But raw, generalized intelligence isn't the whole picture with frontier models in 2026. As developers chase the Pareto frontier— the best-possible confluence of cost and intelligence — Gemini 4 Carbon is also impressively affordable. Its cost per task on the Intelligence Index test is actually slightly cheaper than Gemini 3.8 Flash (high), priced at $7.76 each. That's several dollars cheaper than either Claude Opus 5.5 or Sonnet 5.5, both max with fallback, and not far off half the price of Claude Fable 5.1, which costs $12.99 per task.

Gemini 4 Carbon is still more expensive than OpenAI's GPT 5 Astra and about six times more expensive than GPT 6.1 Sol (max). It's about 13 times as expensive per task as the open-weight DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017, too, although that model measures a mere 32 on the Intelligence Index rankings.

Gemini 4 Carbon isn't the best at everything, but it may be the best at most of the high price points. At least for now. Arena.ai has it holding the top spot on its Pareto Frontier chart at the more expensive end of the spectrum, but at lower price points, the Chinese open-weight models like GLM 5.3-Flash and DeepSeek V4 Pro take over.

Artificial Analysis also highlights that Gemini 4 Carbon isn't as good as Claude Code with Claude Opus 5.5 at programming, and it falls behind Grok 4.7, Muse Wonder, and Kimi K3.1 Pro on knowledge work.

But perhaps the most impressive score is Gemini 4's 54 on the AA-Omniscience Index, which tracks knowledge and hallucinations, beating out the competition by often significant margins.

Gemini 4 Carbon costs $7.76 per Intelligence Index task, ~23% less than Claude Opus 5.5 (max, $10.04) but ~1.2x GPT-6 Astra (max, $6.25). It uses 123k output tokens per task, 29% fewer than Claude Opus 5.5 (max, 173k). At the same $4/$20 standard pricing, its Cost per Task is in line with Gemini 4 Argon ($7.68), while scoring 9 points higher on the Intelligence Index.October 28, 2026

In Google's announcement of the new Gemini 4 model, it talked up its collaboration with the U.S. government for model screening and pre-release testing. In a similar manner to the way Anthropic handled Mythos' release in 2025, Google is rolling out access to Gemini 4 Carbon at a slower pace.

"We're taking a deliberate approach to rollout and are actively engaged in the U.S. government’s voluntary process for pre-release model testing," Google's launch statement reads.

To that end, it's opening up Carbon to a subset of "cyber defenders," and will gradually make the model more widely available through trusted partners through its Fairwind Program. Before it makes Carbon available to developers, it wants to iterate on its guardrails to make the AI safer and more effective for cybersecurity purposes, making it harder for nefarious actors to use it for exploits and hacking.

Where the idea of slowing the industry and introducing greater oversight for model development has risen and fallen in the estimations of the major tech companies, it does appear to be cementing itself as more of a foundational idea for future model releases. Google talks up its release on the improved safety and security of its model, highlighting that it thwarted all attempts from the Gray Swan Indirect Prompt Injection benchmark to have it do something it shouldn't.

Google claims Carbon cuts unsupported claims in its responses twice as much as the Gemini 4 Argon model it debuted just two weeks ago. If it can't complete an action, it will just not do so, rather than fabricating an answer or hedging around missing evidence, Google claims.

It also suggests Carbon will present sources and suggested actions to the user more readily, rather than pursuing its own intentions when there are discrepancies or uncertainties, allegedly cutting model overreach by 26%.

As governments and companies look to make realistic use of AI, tracking usage, managing token budgets, and preventing workers from creating new exploit paths through overuse, a model's safety, security, and reliability will be paramount.

Gemini 4 Carbon scores 54 on AA-Omniscience, the highest of any model, 6 points ahead of Claude Opus 5.5 (max, 48). It answers more questions correctly than Gemini 4 Argon (58% vs 48% accuracy), at the cost of a higher hallucination rate (11% vs 4%). This is still far below Claude Opus 5.5 (max) and GPT-6 Astra (max), both at 50%October 28, 2026

For the past year, a clear focus from frontier and open-weight model developers has been on efficiency and cost. While raising intelligence and improving capabilities, particularly through agentic workflows, has seen great development alongside pomp and circumstance, many of the major announcements and developments have also considered cost a clear component.

That's not likely to change, with the latest research from Gartner showing that just 3% of companies are actually achieving cost and productivity improvements from AI use. But as organizations push to find the real value in AI, maximize token budgets, and try to empower workers who have found improvements from using AI, they'll want consistency.

Tracking return on investment with AI is notoriously difficult, with hallucinations making that far harder. If Google can sell its latest model(s) as the safest and least likely to hallucinate, and do so with high intelligence at a relatively low cost, that might be the new winning formula.

#10360. Announcing DPoP Support for Google OAuth: Securing Refresh Tokens

Traditional OAuth bearer tokens operate on a "finders-keepers" model, making them vulnerable to Infostealer malware (e.g. RedLine, NodeStealer) and Adversary-in-the-Middle (AiTM) attacks.

DPoP (Demonstrating Proof-of-Possession) is an application-layer OAuth 2.0 security profile (RFC 9449) that neutralizes token theft and replay attacks by cryptographically binding tokens to a client-generated asymmetric keypair at the application layer, ensuring that even if a token is stolen, it cannot be replayed without the corresponding private key.

This article is meant to inform you on where and how to adopt DPoP and describes Google's specific DPoP implementation. A DPoP adoption SKILL.md is also available to help implement DPoP.

Some optional features and implementation decisions in RFC 9449 are delegated to Google as an authorization provider. Google Identity, and its OAuth Platform, function as a developer-facing service where engineers implement OAuth 2.0 and cryptographic security controls like DPoP to protect API integrations. Separately, Google Workspace Context-Aware Access (CAA) leverages DPoP to secure enterprise AI workloads on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform by cryptographically validating agent identities at the Agent Gateway.

Google's OAuth Platform supports DPoP binding specifically for refresh tokens. This design choice reflects a pragmatic engineering tradeoff: by sender-constraining the long-lived refresh token, we neutralize the primary vector for persistent offline impersonation. Meanwhile, short-lived access tokens remain standard Bearer tokens, ensuring that high-QPS resource APIs do not incur the per-request asymmetric cryptographic verification overhead of DPoP. This approach delivers robust protection for your most sensitive credentials while maintaining the high performance and low latency of Google API integrations.

Unlike standard Bearer token flows, DPoP requires client-side cryptographic key generation, strict timestamp and nonce handling, specific claim computation (htm, htu, iat, jti), exact header construction, and DPoP proof JWT construction. These changes may introduce additional system constraints to support secure computation, handling, and storage.

The IETF’s Best Current Practice for OAuth 2.0 Security (RFC 9700) discusses sender-constrained refresh tokens. Additionally, the OAuth 2.0 for Browser-Based Applications IETF draft discusses three models: Backend-for-Frontend (BFF), Token-Mediating Backend, and Browser-based OAuth Client.

Following this guidance, Google recommends using DPoP to sender-contrain refresh tokens in these models.

Unlike transport-layer token binding (e.g., mTLS/RFC 8705) which often breaks or terminates at modern cloud proxies and load balancers, DPoP operates at the HTTP header level. This makes it highly resilient for complex, multi-hop environments, including emerging Model Context Protocol (MCP) and autonomous agent architectures.

A common question from security architects is: If DPoP is designed to sender-constrain tokens, why are Google's access tokens still standard Bearer tokens?

The answer lies in operational scaling and performance at Google's scale. Verifying asymmetric cryptographic signatures (such as ES256) requires significant CPU cycles. If every API call to high-throughput Google resource servers (like Gmail, Calendar, or Drive APIs) required verifying a DPoP proof, the compute overhead at the API Gateway layer would be massive, introducing measurable latency and scaling costs.

By constraining DPoP binding strictly to the refresh token, Google's architecture achieves a balance:

Long-Lived Credentials Protected: The highly sensitive, long-lived refresh token (which can be used to obtain new access tokens indefinitely) is cryptographically bound to the client's key pair. If exfiltrated, it is useless to an attacker.

High-Performance Serving: Short-lived access tokens (typically valid for 1 hour or less) remain standard Bearer tokens. This ensures that the vast majority of API requests incur zero cryptographic verification overhead, maintaining the high performance and low latency developers expect from Google APIs.

DPoP is a powerful tool for securing OAuth 2.0 tokens, but it is important to understand its boundaries.

As autonomous AI agents and Model Context Protocol (MCP) architectures emerge, securing tool-use tokens becomes critical. While DPoP is highly resilient in single-hop client-to-server connections, multi-agent workflows (where Agent A delegates a task to Agent B, which calls Resource C) introduce delegation challenges.

In a multi-agent chain, Agent B cannot sign a DPoP proof on behalf of Agent A without access to Agent A's private key—and sharing private keys violates the core security principles of DPoP. Because Google's core consumer OAuth endpoint (oauth2.googleapis.com) does not support OAuth 2.0 Token Exchange (RFC 8693), developers building multi-agent or federated cloud workloads should consider Google Cloud Secure Token Service (STS). By routing delegation through STS, the intermediate agent can exchange the upstream token for a new, short-lived federated token cryptographically bound to its own local key pair, preserving the sender-constrained security model across the entire agent chain without key exposure.

See our DPoP Adoption Guide, our DPoP adoption SKILL.md, and improve your platform security today.

#10357. Redmagic 12 Pro review: You’ve never gamed on a phone like this before

The Redmagic 12 Pro impresses with a Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 processor, liquid cooling, a fan, plus a massive 8,400mAh battery to keep the games going for hours and hours.

As I sat in my living room with my family, playing Monster Hunter Outlanders together on the Redmagic 12 Pro, I completely forgot I was playing on a phone. The game looked perfect. It ran at a locked 60FPS and hadn’t dropped frames since I began playing over an hour ago. It just felt like I was back in college, playing Monster Hunter Freedom on my PSP with my friends in the same room.

Most other phones at this point would have throttled hard. What began at 60FPS almost certainly would have been 40FPS tops after 30 minutes or so; the frame drops during heavy fights would have been even worse, but the Redmagic 12 Pro didn’t skip a beat. Even after a solid hour of playing, the battery had only dropped 10%, completely erasing all concerns about using my phone as a gaming console.

This thing is the real deal, and while I’ve used plenty of high-performance phones and gaming phones over the years, none have quite nailed it as this one has. Now, with my Redmagic 12 Pro and my trusty GameSir G8 Galileo, I can game whenever and wherever I want without battery anxiety or thermal problems. Redmagic has gotten really good at this gaming thing.

Sustained gaming performance is difficult to pull off on a phone, which is why the Redmagic 12 Pro features two types of active cooling: a fan and liquid cooling. This design builds on last year’s with a larger liquid cooling system and a more efficient fan. The intake and exhaust vents are on the sides of the phone, not the back, ensuring these shouldn’t get easily blocked. Despite this, the phone is IP68 and IP69 rated.

Most modern phones have huge camera islands that make them awkward to use in controllers, but the Redmagic 12 Pro features a completely flat build that fits perfectly in *any* controller. I don’t usually like overly flat builds, but it actually suits this phone’s purpose.

This should mean the phone does exceedingly well in benchmarks, but I have the distinct feeling the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 processor inside is making that overly difficult. I don’t have any other SD8 Elite Gen 6 phones just yet, but rumor has it that this is one *hot* chipset. I saw the phone hit 50C during testing, so that definitely seems to be the case.

In 3DMark Solar Bay Extreme, a benchmark that stresses ray-tracing performance over 20 minutes, the Redmagic 12 Pro started at 2065 points and ended at 1169 on its last run. That’s a 43% drop in performance over a 20-minute period, which is in line with passively-cooled phones like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Regardless, the initial performance is nearly double the S26 Ultra, while the 1169 end score matches the S26 Ultra’s best score and is roughly 35% higher than the S26 Ultra’s sustained performance score of around 860.

Actual gaming seemed to fare better in my testing, where I saw sustained performance consistently outperform any Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 phone I have in my possession. When running Wanderstop through the phone’s built-in Steam emulation (more on that soon), the Redmagic 12 Pro averaged 72FPS at the highest settings. Other phones with SD8 Elite Gen 5 processors scored half that, at best. Native Android games like Monster Hunter Outlanders worked even better.

But it’s not just performance that sets this phone apart; it’s the gaming features. Redmagic has fully leaned into the mobile gaming power user angle with its software. Pressing and holding the red button on the right side of the phone launches Gravity X, the built-in game launcher that keeps all your games in one place. It even features an embedded Steam emulator, so all you need to do is sign in with your Steam account and download anything in your library.

Overall performance on Steam games is impressive, but like any emulation, not everything is going to work. Hogwarts Legacy worked near-flawlessly at medium settings, but titles like Star Wars Galactic Racer wouldn’t run at all. Other apps that run Steam games, like DroidDeck and Game Native, also work, but the Adreno 850 GPU doesn’t have official support on those apps yet, so compatibility is very hit or miss (mostly miss) for now.

The Redmagic 12 Pro’s in-game menu is also unlike anything you’ve ever seen. It’s not just jam-packed with information, but has a boatload of bespoke features. One custom mapping tool lets you map the triggers (yes, this phone has capacitive triggers on the side) to any on-screen virtual key.

Another utility records all the time, Xbox/PS5/Switch style, and saves a clip at the touch of a floating button. Yet another features on on-screen AI anime character that can interact with your games and even give you gameplay feedback. You can even enable frame gen and Super Resolution in any game. It’s wild.

All this is wrapped up in an incredibly impressive hardware package, with loud, clear speakers, an excellent 185Hz AMOLED display (that uses DC dimming and 2160Hz PWM for eye comfort), with 5,000-nit peak brightness. I measured the actual HDR brightness at 2300-nits, so this can get nice and bright when it needs to.

If there was ever a phone that could play games and still get you through a full day’s normal phone usage, it’s this one. Passthrough charging is also supported, too, so you can keep the battery from getting overcharged or overheated while gaming for hours.

As is the case with many laser-focused devices like this, the camera is the low point of the experience. It’s got a 50MP rear camera and a 50MP ultra wide. Both get the job done, but they’ll never impress you.

The 16MP 9th-generation under display camera looks impressive on the surface — you can’t see it at all, even with light shining off the display — but the results are still far from good. Redmagic uses an AI algorithm to substantially enhance the overall quality, which cleans up the image nicely but exhibits lots of hallucinations. Just look at my eyes in the above picture. It’s not good.

Thankfully, the Android 17-based Redmagic OS 12 software is quite good. Visually, it reminds me of a mix of AOSP and Samsung’s One UI. Lots of expected features are here — an AI wallpaper generator, call screening, recording with transcripts, writing help, etc — plus all the awesome gaming-focused tools and quite a bit of customization across the OS.

It’s a great overall experience, but Redmagic only offers software updates for 3 years, so this one won’t stay around as long as some other phones. If you’re more interested in mobile gaming than anything — especially if you want a phone that can easily run Steam games in addition to mobile stuff — this is an impressively good choice.

Cloud providers have been told that they must sell 51% of their services to non-Chinese companies for the first time.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia has purportedly told its data center customers that they must limit the amount of business that they do with Chinese companies in what is a new policy for the U.S. company. The move would mean that data center operators who sell their services to Chinese companies must now track what proportion of their overall business they make up, limiting them to just 49% of the total and ensuring 51% are non-Chinese outfits. The FT reports the new policy has been communicated to Nvidia's customers by its sales force and regional teams, and does not appear to have been laid out in any official form via documentation.

Notably, Nvidia's alleged new stance on Chinese customers does not only apply to data centers that are located in Asia. Instead, it applies to those based globally, and even then it only affects new and future contracts rather than any existing Nvidia hardware leasing agreements that are in place. In a statement, Nvidia notes that "American chips are the foundation that American model and application makers need to compete worldwide." The statement goes on to say that current U.S. policy "encourages" cloud providers to build on American infrastructure and to accept business from "everywhere they can."

But this latest move to restrict Chinese access to Nvidia's high-performance data center hardware comes amid ongoing tensions between the United States and China with regard to AI capabilities. Nvidia can only sell its chips to whitelisted companies, a situation that has led to a thriving black market for its wares. Earlier this year, it was found that even Nvidia's older servers have become so sought-after that their values have tripled.

Whether limitations on who Nvidia can sell its chips to have had the desired effect is open to debate, with even China's state-owned telecoms giant China Telecom having recently been found to have Nvidia hardware in its data centers. The situation wouldn't be out of place in a blockbuster espionage thriller, with Nvidia CEO Jason Huang reportedly the target of a spy plot by Chinese agents.

The reasons behind the U.S. government's attempts to limit China's access to Nvidia hardware are obvious. The aim is to stymie China's ability to advance its own AI technologies, with Nvidia chips the go-to option for AI servers and data centers around the globe. But this limitation on which customers cloud service providers can accept will put additional strain on companies that are already fighting it out for the same contracts. Reducing the pool of potential customers could potentially see a shrinking in the scale of data center operations moving forward.
