#10352. How to Optimize AI-Generated Images for Faster Websites

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming a common part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product illustrations, landing pages, blog posts, and digital interfaces. However, generating an image is only one part of the process. Before publishing it, developers need to ensure that the image loads quickly, displays correctly across devices, and maintains its visual quality.

Large image files can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay the appearance of important content. These problems can affect user experience and website performance, particularly on mobile devices and slower internet connections.

A reliable image optimization workflow helps address these challenges. By selecting appropriate dimensions, choosing efficient image formats, automating image processing, and implementing responsive delivery, developers can improve loading performance without sacrificing visual quality.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques and development tools.

Images often account for a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. Uploading large, unoptimized images can make pages slower and increase the amount of data visitors need to download.

Several common problems contribute to poor image performance:

Incorrect dimensions waste bandwidth on smaller screens.

Missing image dimensions can cause unexpected layout shifts.

Poor responsive configuration can make browsers download unnecessarily large images.

Incorrect loading priorities can delay important visual content.

These issues can affect Core Web Vitals, including Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

For developers, image optimization should therefore be part of the website development workflow rather than a final task performed immediately before publication.

Before optimizing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small interface graphic.

For example, a website might use the following dimensions:

These dimensions are examples rather than fixed requirements. The appropriate size depends on the website layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

When preparing source visuals, developers can explore different AI image generation options and consider how the generated images will fit their intended layouts.

Once the image is ready, keep a high-quality master file. This allows developers to create smaller versions for different screen sizes without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Avoid enlarging small images unnecessarily because upscaling can introduce blur and visible artifacts.

Choosing the right format can reduce image size while preserving acceptable visual quality.

Three commonly used formats are AVIF, WebP, and JPEG.

AVIF: Often provides efficient compression for photographs and detailed illustrations. It can significantly reduce file sizes, although encoding may take longer.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation. It offers broad browser support and is suitable for many website images.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and situations where compatibility with older software or systems is important.

PNG is appropriate for certain lossless graphics, screenshots, and images that require transparency. However, it can produce unnecessarily large files when used for photographic content.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare file sizes and inspect visual quality before selecting the final output.

A practical approach is to generate AVIF and WebP versions and provide a JPEG fallback where necessary.

Manually resizing and converting images becomes time-consuming when a website contains dozens or hundreds of visual assets.

Automation makes the process more consistent. Developers can use Sharp, a Node.js image-processing library, to resize images, convert formats, and generate multiple versions from a single source file.

Next, create a file named build-images.mjs:

The script creates multiple image sizes in both WebP and AVIF formats.

The quality settings are starting points, not universal recommendations. Developers should adjust them after checking the resulting file sizes and visual details.

This approach is particularly useful for content-heavy websites, product catalogs, and applications that publish images regularly.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on the visitor's screen and the space available in the layout.

HTML provides two important attributes for this purpose: srcset and sizes.

The srcset attribute lists the available image files and their widths. The sizes attribute describes how wide the image is expected to appear at different viewport sizes.

In this example, the image is expected to occupy approximately 720 pixels on wider screens and nearly the full viewport width on smaller screens.

The browser uses this information to choose an appropriate image candidate.

One common mistake is setting sizes="100vw" for an image that occupies only a small part of the page. This can encourage the browser to download a larger file than necessary.

Always ensure that the sizes attribute reflects the actual website layout.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide multiple image formats and a fallback option.

The browser selects a supported format, while the img element provides the fallback image and alternative text.

Developers should verify that every referenced file exists and that its dimensions match the intended layout.

The picture element can also support different crops for desktop and mobile devices. This is useful when a wide banner needs a separate composition for smaller screens.

Images without defined dimensions can cause content to move while the browser loads the file. This behavior contributes to Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Including width and height attributes helps browsers reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

For responsive layouts, developers can use CSS:

This allows images to scale within their containers while maintaining their proportions.

Images below the initial viewport can generally use native lazy loading:

However, the main hero image should not normally be lazy-loaded when it is responsible for LCP. Delaying an important above-the-fold image can make the page appear slower.

Where appropriate, developers can use fetchpriority="high" for the main image to help the browser prioritize its download.

The correct strategy depends on the image's position and importance within the page.

Image optimization involves more than file size and dimensions. Developers should also consider accessibility, file organization, and metadata.

Alternative text helps people using screen readers understand meaningful images. It should describe the image's purpose in the context of the page.

Decorative images that provide no useful information should generally use an empty alt attribute.

File names should also be descriptive. Names such as home-office-sunrise.webp are easier to understand and maintain than generic names such as image-final-v3.png.

Metadata requires additional consideration. Image-processing tools may remove metadata by default to reduce file size. This can include information that a project needs to preserve, depending on its requirements.

Before removing metadata, developers should review whether the website or client requires attribution, provenance information, or other embedded details.

After implementing an optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Chrome DevTools provides useful information about image requests.

Open Developer Tools and select the Network panel.

Resize the viewport or use device emulation.

Check whether the browser downloads appropriately sized images.

Developers can also inspect the image selected by the browser through the console:

This returns the current image URL selected for that element.

For websites with multiple images, inspect the relevant elements individually.

Finally, run an audit using Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. These tools can identify issues involving oversized images, image formats, loading priorities, and other performance factors.

Image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, JavaScript, and third-party scripts can also influence loading speed.

Several mistakes can reduce the effectiveness of an image optimization workflow:

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one format always produces the smallest file.

Omitting the sizes attribute from responsive images.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Applying excessive compression and damaging visual details.

Removing metadata without checking project requirements.

Measuring file size without inspecting the final visual quality.

A consistent testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but the results depend on the image, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best way to choose an appropriate option.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of the page should generally load promptly.

The fundamental techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, AI-generated visuals may contain complex textures, gradients, and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design and make it easier to create custom visual content. However, efficient delivery requires more than exporting an image and uploading it to a website.

Developers should choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive image delivery. Including image dimensions helps prevent layout shifts, while appropriate loading priorities can improve the experience for visitors.

By testing images across different devices and reviewing their actual performance, development teams can maintain visual quality while reducing unnecessary downloads.

A repeatable optimization workflow makes it easier to publish new visuals without sacrificing website speed, accessibility, or reliability.

#10349. Bambu Lab H3C 3D printer review: A nine-tool monster

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Bambu Lab H3C is an excellent nine-tool 3D printer that printed everything we could throw at it. Whether you want nine-color PLA models, mix and match engineering filaments, or crazy squishable TPU shoes in a riot of color, this machine is a beast. If you don’t have low ceilings or a fear of step ladders, the H3C might be the only printer you’ll ever need.

Works with multiple materials and nozzle sizes in the same print

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

The Bambu Lab H3C is the printer we’ve been waiting for: a true Bambu tool changer. It has nine “Swaptek” tools that use a technology similar to the H2C Vortek rack, except each nozzle is built into a lightweight, hardwired tool with a dedicated filament path. The H3C is Bambu’s fastest multicolor 3D printer, and though it reduces waste, it does not eliminate it.

The H3C comes with a surprising “old school” technology: the ability to print 2.85mm filament. This is in response to China’s increasing fascination with 3D-printed shoes made with a wide variety of squishy TPU. Bambu sent along a few spools of their new super soft 75A TPU to show off, and it does make for comfy footwear. Each unit comes with one special 2.85mm tool as a standard feature, though you can buy a “normal” 1.75mm tool for $59 if you’re not into the novelty of phat filament.

You will also be able to add cutting, drawing, embossing, and foil transfer tools to the system. These were not available at the time of review, but we’ve seen these accessories on the H2D and A2L. The 3D printer will NOT come with a laser variant.

Bambu is checking off yet another item from the user wish list: the ability to print with more than one nozzle size. Because this is more of a software upgrade, you’ll also gain the ability to mix nozzles with Bambu’s other dual nozzle systems: the H2D, H2C, and X2D. The H2D will get the ability first, and the other printers will be updated by March.

The H3C has a 330 x 320 x 325mm build volume (the same as the H2C) and a massive “AMS” type module perched atop the main unit that holds nine spools. This gives it a somewhat towering mushroom shape that’s over three feet tall. It did not fit on my studio counter, which is a standard-sized IKEA kitchen countertop with a filament rack three feet off the surface. When we parked it on my industrial-grade shelving unit next to the H2D, even my 6’4” husband needed a step stool to load filament. Bambu was prepared for this problem and created a special rolling cart for the machine to make it more accessible. This is the first time the company has offered custom furniture for one of their printers and is an optional purchase that I highly recommend.

All nine tools are integrated into the bottom of the AMS 3H, which means the H3C only comes as a nine-tool 3D printer. There is no such thing as a single-color H3C. If nine colors aren’t enough for you, the printer can be hooked up to as many as four “normal” AMS units with the help of an optional buffer. Unfortunately, all outside AMS units need to run through the 5th tool slot, so you will lose the purge-free perk of the H3C.

The AMS 3H does not have motorized rollers, as retraction is unneeded on a tool changer. Seven of the slots have a TPU bypass, something we first saw on the single spool AMS HT. Two of the slots are nestled within an interior chamber for drying at up to 85°C. Though you can only dry two spools in the AMS 3H, you can run the dryer while printing, as it has its own power supply. The sealed dryer does not “leak” enough heat to dry the rest of the chamber…we checked. But that also means you don’t have to worry about the dryer melting a spool of PLA left in a nearby slot.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The FlexFeed extruder mounted on the toolhead is amazing. It can self-adjust to handle normal 1.75mm PLA or expand to push 2.85mm TPU quickly and without issue. The Bambu Lab H3C comes standard with eight .4mm nozzles for 1.75mm filament and one .4mm nozzle for 2.85mm filament. The nozzles are held in place with three screws and are pretty easy to swap out. You do need an entirely separate tool (with larger openings) for the 2.85mm filament. Because the H3C can print with multiple nozzle sizes, you could leave one or more tools dedicated to .2, .6, or .8 nozzle sizes if that fits your workflow.

For example, slots 6 and 8 are located inside the dryer and have a TPU bypass. You could dedicate a .6mm nozzle to these tools to better handle TPU, something like PA CF, which both benefit from toasty dry filament and an extra-wide nozzle. All nozzles for the H3C are hardened steel (HRC 55+) by default.

My H3C did have some pre-launch issues and is running Beta firmware that was updated several times during the review. The printer developed an annoying clicking sound coming from the left-side stepper motor, and one of the tools has a tendency to tangle and catch on the other Bowden tubes, causing it to dismount unexpectedly – and annoyingly – during long multicolor prints. I printed a few TPU spacers to try and solve this problem without much success as of yet.

The Bambu Lab H3C is launching with a retail price starting at $2,499 for all nine tools (eight standard 1.75mm and one 2.85mm). This is only $100 more than the launch price of the H2C. Additional tools and nozzle sizes will be available, though we do not have all the pricing at this time. A bundle that includes the Bambu Station (rolling cart) starts at $2,799. This makes the machine a bit on the spendy side, but for a helmet-class, honest-to-goodness tool changer with Bambu Lab’s ease of use, this 3D printer has earned its place on our Best 3D Printer of 2026 list.

330 x 320 x 325 mm (12.99 x 12.59 x 12.79 inches)

PLA/TPU/ABS/ASA/PA/PC/PET/PPS/Fiber reinforced (up to 350 degrees)

PEI textured spring steel sheet (120 degrees)

493 x 571 x 1016 mm (19.40 x 22.48 x 40 inches)

The Bambu Lab H3C comes with a toolkit and the cables needed to assemble your 3D printer, as well as a few spare parts. The printer is preloaded with files, including a scraper that you can build yourself with the included parts. There’s also a quick start guide for both the base unit and the AMS 3H.

For the review, Bambu Lab provided spare printer tools for both 1.75mm and 2.85mm filaments, as well as several spools of PLA Palette and 2.85mm TPU 75A. We also received a Bambu Station for the review.

The H3C is an incredible piece of hardware. The Swaptek system has nine tools, each with its own filament path, heater, and hardened steel nozzle. The FlexFeed extruder has cooling and everything else. Each nozzle stays loaded and is preheated for lightning-fast filament swaps. I clocked it at six seconds from printing one color to printing the next.

The tools and nozzles come in both 1.75 mm and 2.85 mm filament sizes. Each tool has a nozzle attached by three screws, which can be swapped with different sizes. The H3C ships with one 2.85mm tool, but you have the option to purchase more tools if you want to customize your configuration. Though not ready at the time of this review, Bambu Lab will be offering tools for cutting, drawing, debossing, and foil transfer at a later date.

The tools dock on a front-mounted rack that retracts, allowing the build plate to be viewed from the front of the printer. This is a not-so-subtle jab at Prusa’s INDX, which has tools mounted to a fixed rack that one must peer through to see the first layer printing. It adds a layer of unneeded complexity, but it also looks pretty cool in action.

The photo below shows all nine tools lowered, using a maintenance setting. Normally, only one tool lowers at a time while in use.

Unlike previous Bambu Lab 3D printers, the AMS is required, not optional. Instead of a box that can be parked here or there, the AMS 3H is designed to screw into the top of the H3C. The plastic sides have a weird organic shape, but are actually quite practical – hidden side panels pop out for maintenance, allowing you to reach into the machine. The massive lid can be propped open, which is good because it’s most likely going to be over your head.

The tools ship attached to the bottom of the AMS. Though the H3C has the same build volume, and the same length and width as the H2 line, it is doubtful that an H2 can be upgraded to an H3C by simply plopping an AMS 3H on top of an older machine.

The AMS 3H has a separate chamber with a magnetically attached lid covering slots 6 and 8, which can dry filament at up to 85°C. All filament slots except 2 and 4 can bypass the AMS feeders through a hole in the bottom to directly feed TPU to the tools. The spool slots are numbered in a zigzag method, with 2,4,6 and 8 on the back row and 1, 3, 5, 7, and 9 on the front. All the filament feeders are in the middle of the two rows, with the even-numbered spools feeding forward like always, and the odd spools feeding backward. It can get a bit complicated if you’re not using Bambu-branded filament with RFID tags. Being on the short side, I have to use a step stool to see the feeders.

The body of the Bambu Lab H3C looks similar to the rest of the H series except for the toolhead. The Flexfeed extruder can adjust automatically to push multiple filament widths and does well with some of the softest TPU I’ve tried.

The H3C has active chamber heating, up to 65°C, goes to 350°C at the hot end and 120°C on the bed, which makes printing PPA and PPS, nylons, ABS, and ASA easy. For the smellier filaments, it has active carbon and HEPA filtration.

The ventilation is sufficient to run lower-temperature filaments like PLA with the printer’s door closed.

The printer has an excellent camera that is used for AI detection on prints and can be monitored from the Bambu Handy app as well as the device page of Bambu Slicer. The camera can also capture time-lapses of prints when a USB stick is inserted in the slot behind the touch screen.

The Bambu Lab H3C has a footprint similar to the other H series printers but does require space on both sides to access the side panels of the AMS 3H. With the top-mounted AMS and the space required for the tool wiring and Bowden tubes, the Bambu Lab H3C requires a total of 1306 mm in height (over four feet) to operate comfortably. When resting on a standard-height counter or the 3ft tall racks we normally use for our printers, I needed a small ladder to reach the rear spools in the AMS 3H. Even my husband, who is 6’4,” had to stretch to reach the back. Shortly after I had the printer set up, I received the Bambu Station, which, at 23.5 inches tall on its wheels, is a great solution for keeping this printer in reach. If you don’t have a shelf or table the height of a coffee table, I recommend getting one.

Bambu is also introducing a companion E1 Air Purifier that syncs with the H3C and activates when you start a print job. We did not receive an E1 to test, and we’re not sure if the air cleaner will be offered as a standalone product. It is part of the premium $3,049 combo that includes the printer, rolling cart, and several spools of filament.

The Bambu Lab H3C arrives in two large assemblies: the main printer and all the things attached to the AMS 3H. After removing the packing material, zip ties holding the tool head in place, four screws holding the bed in place, and two retainers holding the rack in place, the AMS 3H can be hefted on top of the base unit. This is best done while the unit is on the floor or at least somewhere with a bunch of overhead clearance.

The touch screen attaches to the base unit without tools, and the ribbon cable is just long enough to reach the back of the screen. The nine tools have to be manually placed in the front rack, which takes some time until you learn how they attach. At least one needed to be attached once the printer was powered up.

The AMS 3H has its own power cord for the heater and a cable connecting it to the base. There are preinstalled screws to attach the Base unit to the AMS 3H, but I left these unused since removing the front panel requires tipping the AMS 3H back a bit.

The Bambu Lab H3C performs a bed level as part of its initial calibration suite and again before each print. This works flawlessly in practice.

When using non-TPU 1.75mm filament, the AMS 3H loads filament just like a regular AMS. Filament is inserted into the feeder, which automatically pulls it into the toolhead when that tool is selected. The RFID tag on Bambu Lab branded filament will automatically enter the filament type and color. Other filament brands need to be entered manually into the printer via the touchscreen.

The AMS 3H does not have powered rollers to retract the filament. You will need to select Unload from the filament screen, and the printer will cut the filament and retract it from the tool head. You will need to manually pull the filament out the rest of the way.

2.85mm filament and softer TPU will need to be manually fed through a port on the bottom of the AMS 3H. All filament slots except 2 and 4 have this port. The filament bypasses the tubing to feed directly into the tools. The standard Bowden tube must be removed from the tool and inserted into a holder on the side. For 1.75mm TPU, a small piece of Bowden tube is inserted into the top of the tool to help feed the filament correctly. 2.85mm filament can be inserted directly into the tool. Once inserted, filament using the bypass must be loaded manually.

Bambu Studio, based on PrusaSlicer and its forbear, is one of the best slicers out there. Working seamlessly with the printer for filament mapping, control, monitoring, and access to all of the files on Makerworld, it is a joy to use.

The H3C is a much faster machine than its predecessors because it does not need to reel filament back and forth from the AMS. However, it still uses a purge tower, which can waste a bit of time and filament. It only takes about six seconds to swap tools, which is quite remarkable.

The H3C did not come with filament samples, but Bambu Lab did send along several spools of PLA Palette and spools of 2.85 75A TPU to try. You’re going to need a bunch of filament for this beast, so check out our list of best filaments for 3D printing for suggestions.

To get the measure of the Bambu Lab H3C, I loaded up one of my favorite prints, a steampunk dragon by the designer Cinderwing 3D. I had previously printed it in eight PLA colors using two AMS modules on an Anycubic Kobra 3 Max, which is a bed slinger. That first print took 37 hours and 28 minutes and wasted 267g of filament in poop and purge.

Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 140mm/s, the H3C cut that time down to a bit less than 24 hours, and only wasted 68g in its purge tower. The print quality is excellent, and all the colors came out clean. The main color is Prusa Pearl Mouse, Polymaker Panchroma Silver, and black, white, magenta, yellow, and cyan from Bambu’s new Palette PLA, which is actually designed for color mixing.

Eight colors were not enough. I swapped out the 2.85mm tool with the spare 1.75mm tool and printed a nine-color Princess Donut and Mongo from Dungeon Crawler Carl (my latest obsession). I have been using this print to test out other tool changers for a future comparison. The print came out fantastic, with super shiny silks and clean color transitions. I had to “hand paint” this in Bambu Slicer, which took a bit of time. The teeth on the print are a bit shaky, but that is due to the insane amount of support this model required.

This printed in 15 hours and 12 minutes, with a 152.6-gram prime tower, using a .2mm layer height and default settings. For comparison, the same print with the same settings but only 5 colors on the H2C took 24 hours and 44 minutes and used 111.4grams on its prime tower. The Prusa INDX took a similar amount of time (15 hours and 38 minutes) to run the same print in 8 colors, but only needed to waste 24grams of prime purge.

Princess Donut (Cat) Polymaker dual silk sunset gold-red, Polymaker Panchroma Silk Purple, Jessie Elixir Electric Lemonade.

Mongo (Dinosaur): Polymaker silk blue, Polymaker white HT-PLA Pro, Bambu pink PLA basic.

Saddle: Inland brown PLA+, Hobby Lobby PLA wood, Elegoo red silk PLA.

The Bambu Lab H3C is one of the best printers I’ve tested for printing TPU. The AMS 3H bypass ports require a tiny bit of set up, but they are much better than dealing with an external spool holder. With nine slots at my disposal, I can always have a spool of TPU ready to go.

My first TPU print was a strap hinge that combines normal 95A TPU with stiff PLA brackets. The designer wove the two filaments together manually, though you can make your own hinges flexible by using “beam interlocking” in the slicer.

I printed this in Polymaker red PLA and Polymaker red TPU 95A and the hinge is extremely strong. Printed with a .2mm layer height and running at an average speed of 115 mm/s the print completed in just over an hour.

I was excited to try the 2.85mm 75A TPU that Bambu Lab sent, and it did not disappoint. The 2.85mm nozzle comes preset to run filament directly from the bypass and just requires manual loading. This is the softest TPU I’ve ever run, and the FlexFeed extruder handled it perfectly.

I first ran the wrist rest from the printer’s memory using Bambu Lab’s green 75A TPU. The print took 50 minutes to run, used only 20g of filament, and is very squishy. I used the same filament and printed some tires for an RC project I’m working on. Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 75mm/s the tires took 57 minutes per set of two. They look nice and are super squishy. They only have 2 walls and 7% concentric infill, so they are extremely lightweight.

For ABS, I ran a two-color Magic 8-ball-style D20. Dice Studio lets you print custom dice with numbers or text and export it as a 3mf file ready to print. This is printed in Ionized Cobalt Black Matte ABS and Natural Matte ABS from Fusion Filaments. The print was very nice, with just a little roughness on the overhangs on the very bottom of the die, and a little roughness on the text where it becomes very small.

To really test out the heated chamber, I used a filament I struggle with: PA6 GF. I printed a Twist Lock Container using grey PA6 GF from Sunlu. I ran it with 3 walls and a .2mm layer height at an average speed of 140mm/s. The print took nearly 6 hours to complete. The result is a nearly indestructible canister that looks good too.

The H3C is quite the multimaterial powerhouse and handles every material equally well. I don’t own any filament that it can’t print well, from soft TPU to PPS. Multiple nozzle sizes and multiple filament sizes can all be used together, and you’ve got nine places to put it all. Unless you need a print farm, I can’t really see the need for a different printer. This is a one-stop shop.

With a retail price of $2,499 for the H3C Tool Changer, this is a printer to definitely put on your wish list. In fact, I’m so impressed with the tool changer aspect, I keep forgetting that it’s also a helmet-class printer, which makes this machine capable of handling nearly any print you need to create.

If the Bambu Lab H3C seems out of reach, you can also check out last year’s Bambu Lab H2C, which is no slouch at multimaterial and has the same build volume. It’s currently on sale for $1,899 with a four-color AMS combo. The Snapmaker U1 is the most affordable tool changer we like on the market right now, and though it is smaller and only four-color, the $699 Black Friday price tag is very tempting. And of course, we have the Prusa CORE One INDX eight-color machine for those who want to keep their printers off the cloud, currently retailing at $1,949 as a kit.

Denise Bertacchi is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware US, covering 3D printing. Denise has been crafting with PCs since she discovered Print Shop had clip art on her Apple IIe. She loves reviewing 3D printers because she can mix all her passions: printing, photography, and writing.

#10346. Hands-on with the DockCase Mount Ling 2 Pro

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Mount Ling 2 Pro offers a lot of flexibility for a gaming dock.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

DockCase is no stranger to Kickstarting unique SSD enclosures and docks, with the company’s Visual Smart M.2 enclosure making its way onto our list of the best SSD enclosures. The Mount Ling 2 Pro, however, is gunning for a spot among the best USB-C docks. It’s a dock for handhelds like the Steam Deck and Switch 2, an external enclosure for an NVMe SSD, and a game capture device, all packaged up in a dock that can show off your handheld and expand your port selection.

There are two versions of the dock: the standard Mount Ling 2 and the Pro version. The only difference between them is the internal capture card. The base version is $109 via an early bird deal while the Kickstarter gets underway, and the Pro version is $149. They’ll be priced at $199 and $249, respectively, after the early deals are gone.

It’s the world’s first “smart creator dock,” says DockCase, which is partially true. There are other docks that give you additional inputs, a stand, and capture capabilities with a handheld, and at a lower price. The AVerMedia X’TRA GO, for instance, offers all of that for $160. The differentiator here is the internal M.2 slot that can support up to 4TB of NVMe storage, with points to mount 2280, 2242, and 2230-sized drives. Of course, this external storage only works with platforms that normally support external storage (so, not the Switch or Switch 2).

For PC-adjacent handhelds running Windows or Linux, the Mount Ling 2 Pro is an expensive but useful dock that gives you capture, storage, and port expandability in one attractive package. For the Switch and Switch 2, the usefulness isn’t as clear. You can’t use the SSD with either platform, and Nintendo’s game of whack-a-mole with third-party accessories means a system update could break HDMI passthrough (and, in the process, the capture function of the dock).

DockCase says the Mount Ling 2 Pro supports the Steam Deck, ROG Ally, Switch, Switch 2, and “more,” presumably wrapping up other Windows- or Linux-based handhelds like the ROG Ally X Xbox or Lenovo Legion Go S. Switch and Switch 2 are supported, as well, though getting Nintendo’s handheld to connect properly isn’t always smooth.

The dock connects to your handheld with a USB 3.2 Gen Type-C connection capable of 10 Gbps, and DockCase includes a small, braided 40 Gbps cable in the box, along with two right-angle connectors. That single connection gives you up to 100W through the USB-C port, along with Gigabit Ethernet, an HDMI 2.0 connection (4K@60Hz), a single USB 3.2 Gen 2 Type-A port, and a USB 2.0 port. An additional USB-C connection serves as another USB 3.2 Gen 2 port on the Mount Ling 2, while it becomes the capture card output on the Pro model.

Interestingly, there’s also an optical audio output available. Although I don’t suspect most users will put it to use (HDMI transfers audio, as well), it’s a nice fallback if you plan on using the dock with a more elaborate audio setup. It’s a nice bonus, though I would’ve liked a 3.5mm audio jack as well, considering handhelds no longer have access to that connection.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The dock itself is constructed almost entirely of plastic, and holding it in your hands, you can tell it’s filled with more air than hardware. It’s a sizable package at 8.4 x 7 x 3.5 inches. Outside of the shell, there’s a thick layer of firm silicone to rest your handheld on. There are grooves to provide a natural resting place for just about any handheld, as well as a deep central cut that fits the Switch and Switch 2 specifically.

You can install your SSD at the bottom of the dock, with mounting points for 2280, 2242, and 2230 drives. There's a single 40mm fan internally, as well as venting around the back and side edges, to keep the drive cool. The fan will automatically adjust its speed out of the box, but you’re free to manually adjust the speed or turn the fan off, if you want.

Unfortunately, there’s no room for a heatsink. The face of the drive sits nearly flush against the vented cover on the bottom of the dock. Even if you left the cover off, most heatsinks would push past the rubber feet and prop the dock up.

Setting up the Mount Ling 2 Pro is mostly a matter of plugging everything in and getting on your way. The dock goes through a connecting phase when you hook up a new device, and it’s finicky. Sometimes it would drop one part of the connection, like charging, while picking up another, particularly with the Switch 2. You want to have the dock fully connected before hooking up your host.

The main page of the dock’s screen provides HDMI, SSD, and power info at a glance, which is helpful to quickly check, for example, that the HDMI passthrough is operating at the correct resolution and refresh rate without digging into the settings. A swipe to the right brings up five quick-access settings, which just take you into the settings menu, while a swipe to the right gives you access to volume and fan controls. The volume adjustment on the dock is passed through to the host.

Swiping up instead gives you the full settings menu, which has more to monitor than adjust. You can see the SSD details (including SMART monitors), the status of USB connections, and your HDMI info, andother details about the dock. For settings, there are different HDMI transfer modes, in case you run into a broken handshake, along with power delivery settings if you need to roll back to PD 2.0.

For the dock itself, you have some small control over the fan and built-in lighting. For the fan, you can adjust the speed manually, turn it off, or leave it on the default automatic mode. The lighting settings are surprisingly bare. You can choose between blue, red, or a gradient between them, and that’s it. Otherwise, you can just dim or turn off the lights. If you find the screen and lights distracting, there’s a dedicated button on the back of the dock to turn all the lights off (including the screen).

Although the screen is responsive with minimal lag between swipes and taps, the software running here isn’t perfect. Particularly with the Switch 2, if I connected things in the wrong order, the dock would force itself to reboot, only sometimes picking up the Switch 2 when it came back. DockCase offers firmware updates over USB, and hopefully it gets a patch between units shipped to fix some of these quirks.

The firmware updates are a nice sign, but they bring up an unfortunate reality of creating a device for the Switch 2 without Nintendo’s seal of approval. Both the Switch and Switch 2 are infamously fickle with third-party docks and hubs. A system update could break compatibility, forcing a firmware update.

DockCase plays up Switch 2 support on its product page, and that is a big draw. However, the dock can’t get around the limitations that Nintendo itself has placed on its handheld. You may as well fill the SSD slot with concrete if you’re docking a Switch 2, and docking the device is troublesome. It took multiple emails for me to get the Switch 2 to pass through its HDMI signal at all. You have to connect everything in a specific way, and critically, have the Switch 2 already unlocked when you plug it into the bottom USB-C port; the top port won’t work.

With the Steam Deck, everything was seamless, and although I didn’t have a Windows handheld on hand to try out, I don’t anticipate there will be major issues. DockCase may offer a lot of functionality in a single box with the Mount Ling Pro 2, but it’s all operating over standard connections that are well supported in the Windows ecosystem.

You can use the SSD with the Steam Deck, though you’ll need to head to desktop mode to partition the drive and add a folder for Steam games. The process is similar on Windows handhelds if you’re using some sort of handheld-native overlay.

For the capture portion, it serves its purpose. Critically, DockCase doesn’t have any software for capturing or recording like the mainstream capture cards you’ll find from Elgato and AVerMedia, so you’ll need to use something else like OBS. For any of these types of recording/streaming platforms, the DockCase shows up as a simple video capture device, with resolution and frame rate determined by your settings on the host device (assuming it’s within that 4K60 limit that HDMI 2.0 is capable of).

There isn’t much of a need here for HDMI 2.1, considering the types of devices connecting to the Mount Ling Pro 2, though it would’ve been a nice upside. You can still push the refresh rate higher, just not at 4K. And in fairness to DockCase, the devices it’s targeting really have no business driving a 4K display.

You shouldn’t expect wonders out of SSD performance with the Mount Ling 2 Pro, given that it uses a USB 3.2 Gen 2 connection that’s only capable of up to 10 Gbps. High performance in an external enclosure is the game of Thunderbolt 5 docks with M.2 storage, which can often reach sequential reads above 5,000 MB/s over the 80 Gbps Thunderbolt 5 interface. Here, the connection will bottleneck your speeds, all the way down to typical PCIe 3.0 NVMe SSDs.

Still, in the context of gaming on a handheld, that isn’t the end of the world. It’s nice to have that much storage on tap for a device like the Steam Deck, which is otherwise only expandable with a larger internal SSmicroSDicro SD card.

In CrystalDiskMark, the Mount Ling 2 Pro landed with sequential reads and writes around 820 MB/s, with random performance far lower. We tested with a 2 TB WD_Black SN770, which is a PCIe 4.0 drive that boasts sequential reads up to 5150 MB/s when installed directly in a motherboard. In the Mount Ling 2 Pro, it behaves more like an external SSD like the SK hynix Beetle X31 2TB, naturally hamstrung by the connection.

The Mount Ling 2 Pro does a lot, combining external storage, a capture card, and an attractive dock into a single package that works with both PC handhelds and the Nintendo Switch 2. The SSD performance isn’t great, as is to be expected out of a dock that uses a 10 Gbps USB link for connection, but that downside isn’t much of a concern considering the devices this dock targets.

Although Switch 2 support is a big upside, it’s also fickle. You don’t get access to the SSD, and the connection can fail if you don’t follow the order of operations for connection. The Mount Ling 2 Pro makes sense if you have a Switch 2 and a PC handheld of some kind, but as a pure Switch 2 dock, it doesn’t make much sense. You’d be better off buying an external capture card for use with the official dock.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10342. Google confirms something was draining Pixel Watch batteries, fix rolling out

Google has confirmed that some Pixel Watches are experiencing unexpected battery drain, but it also says a fix is already available.

Last week, we reported on a trending Pixel Watch complaint – sudden and out-of-the-ordinary battery drain. More than a few posts and comments on sites like Reddit suggested Pixel Watch users were noticing an uptick in battery usage, leaving them with less than half of their normal usage.

Google reached out to 9to5Google with a statement shortly after the issue showed up, noting it was aware of the problem. Yesterday, it stated that a fix for the Pixel Watch battery drain issue is being rolled out now. The update is expected to appear for all users “over the next couple of days.”

The fix for the battery drain issue, affecting a small number of Pixel Watch users has begun rolling-out and will be complete over the next couple of days. To implement the fix, please update your Pixel Watch apps by pressing the crown on your Pixel Watch, opening the Play Store app, and tapping Manage apps > Update all.

Plummeting battery life didn’t affect every single Pixel Watch, but a number of users across models like the Pixel Watch 2 and up were more than enough to suggest something other than hardware was causing the issue.

Google hasn’t commented on where the issue originated, and whether it came from a recent Wear OS update. It’s possible it could have been one of the Pixel Watch’s proprietary apps, or something in general software that demanded too much background energy.

In any case, this software update is expected to fix any unexpected battery drain on Pixel Watch models that were affected, and should be available before your Pixel Watch can run out again.

#10341. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.

AI demand is tightening DRAM and NAND supply, pushing up costs for device makers and leaving less memory available for consumer electronics

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple is about to feel the squeeze from rising memory prices, with the cost of RAM climbing just as the company heads into its busiest shopping season. According to Reuters, Apple expects to pay more for memory in the December quarter, with the CEO, John Ternus, describing the current price increases as “unprecedented. The company has so far managed to soften the blow by using memory bought at lower prices, cutting costs elsewhere and raising prices on some products. However, it is reported that its manufacturing bill has climbed by $33 billion in just a few months.

But that cushion is getting thinner, and Apple expects memory purchases to become even more expensive into 2027. “What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. And as we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. Now for the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs, partly offset by a few factors. So as you know, we changed the price on many of our products, including Mac and iPad at the end of June and iPhone in September, and these price increases provided some offset.” Ternus said during Apple’s November 2 earnings call.

Memory is used across Apple's hardware lineup, so sustained price increases could affect manufacturing cost for several product categories—although Apple has not announced another round of price increases. Absorbing higher components could, however, indefinitely become difficult. Dave Nicholson, Chief Research Officer at the Futurum Group, also identified memory prices as a growing pressure point for Apple's margins and hardware pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. According to him, Apple is in a different position from companies pouring hundreds of billions of dollars into AI data center infrastructure. The impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the eventual returns on massive AI capital expenditure, he said.

The next pressure point for @Apple may not be AI CapEx, but the rising cost of memory across its hardware portfolio. @TheFuturumGroup Chief Research Officer @DaveN007 explained that higher memory prices could put more pressure on Apple’s margins and pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. Even with demand holding up, the question is how much of those higher costs consumers will absorb. And the warnings from Apple's suppliers suggest that the cost pressure is unlikely to ease quickly. Last month, Samsung, a major supplier of RAM(DRAM), NAND flash storage and OLED displays for Apple, said that memory demand would far exceed supply as AI infrastructure continues to expand. Its memory division also reported a quarter-on-quarter increase in DRAM average prices during the third quarter, while NAND prices rose by high-teen percentages. Senior executive vice president of its memory business warned the supply crunch could extend to 2028 and beyond. Elsewhere, SK Hynix delivered a similar warning, saying it had secured long-term agreements covering more than Half of its production capacity for2027 and 2028. Micron, the third major supplier for Apple aside from Samsung and SK Hynix, is also benefiting from the surging demand for memory. Its latest published outlook warns that supply shortages in memory and storage could take “considerable time” to improve.

We have previously reported several signs of the pressure now confronting Apple. In January, the company was already scrambling to secure memory supplies amid strong demand, with then CEO Tim Cook warning that rising prices could have a greater impact on earnings. By June, rising component expenses had translated into higher retail prices. Apple increased prices across several Mac and iPad models, including a $400 increase to the starting price of the MacBook Pro, due to the RAM crisis. In July, Cook described the memory market as a “hundred-year-flood” and said Apple expected to pay more for memory in the September quarter. The company had also increased its inventory to $11.09 billion as it built a buffer against supply and price pressures.

#10338. Microsoft Authenticator is ditching its own password backup for Google One

Microsoft is ditching its own password backup option for the Microsoft Authenticator app for Android and instead using Google One for that service.

Microsoft Authenticator is a one-time password / two-factor authentication app that works more or less like Google Authenticator. The app on your phone spits out a one-time code that you’ll use for logging into various online accounts.

Starting in January, though, Microsoft Authenticator is doing away with Microsoft Account backup for your one-time password connections. Microsoft detailed the change on a support page, explaining that backup on Android will now run through Google One backup. Users will need to toggle on Microsoft Authenticator in Google One’s backup settings on their device.

Starting January 2027, you will no longer use your Microsoft personal account to enable backup. You will need to enable “Microsoft Authenticator” from Google One backup.

It’s an interesting change, to say the least, but it’s not unprecedented. Microsoft Authenticator already relied on iCloud Backup on iOS to back up one-time passwords and accounts.

The change kicks in starting in January of next year, which is mere weeks away at this point.

#10335. Evaluating AI agents: moving past the vibe check

Your agent can loop, burn tokens, or give a completely hallucinated answer and still look fine. A vibe check won't catch that. This post is the playbook behind our AI Agent Clinic episode How to Actually Evaluate (Any) AI Agent: Beyond the Vibe Check, where we build an eval pipeline for a LangGraph agent in 60 minutes. Here's what's inside:

When teams start building an AI agent, testing usually looks the same. You wire up a model, hand it a few tools, run three or four prompts in a terminal, and eyeball the output. If the answer looks reasonable, you ship it.

In practice, this breaks down fast. You tweak a system prompt to fix an edge case in one workflow, and suddenly the agent stops calling a required tool in another.

Worse, agents fail differently than standard software. An agent can get stuck in a retry loop, fire off multiple tool calls, yet still return a polite, plausible-sounding final answer.

In production, that hidden failure is costly:

Integrating a reliable evaluation suite into your development lifecycle can seem like extra overhead during initial prototyping, right up until modifying your system prompt causes unexpected breaking changes. The key is recognizing that evaluation is a progressive journey: any repeatable evaluation system (no matter how simple) is better than relying on ad-hoc spot checks or no system at all. You don't need an elaborate automated grading architecture on Day 0. Instead, start with simple, repeatable checks and evolve your evaluation into a more complex, automated framework as your agent's capabilities and production demands grow.

Establishing a repeatable evaluation system is made substantially easier by integrating these decisions into an evaluation framework. Transitioning beyond simple subjective checks relies on two core pillars: human oversight and targeted tools. In this article, we explore how to set up an evaluation framework using the open-source agent-eval toolkit. We'll demonstrate how combining manually reviewed traces with automated verification helps detect silent regressions early in development and scales seamlessly as your agent transitions to production.

Before writing test code, you need a shared vision of what a good outcome actually looks like. Anchor your tests in critical user journeys (CUJs) rather than abstract benchmarks.

A CUJ defines what a user expects the system to achieve, structured around their persona, goals, constraints, and verification conditions:

Every clause in that statement becomes an evaluation point: Did the agent resolve the correct transaction ID? Did it check the ledger before drafting? Did it map internal IDs back to the external nomenclature?

Defining these expectations upfront forces product stakeholders, downstream users, and engineers to agree on defining how “success” is measured.

You do not need six months of production logs to build your initial golden dataset. Instead, break each CUJ down into concrete scenarios (both happy paths and edge cases) and feed them into a user simulator LLM (we like ADK user simulations). Primed with a persona and behavioral constraints (for example, an impatient analyst giving incomplete ticket numbers, or a developer asking a multi-part debugging question), the simulator mirrors the messy phrasing, typos, and multi-turn complexity of real users.

By testing your agent against a synthetic user and realistic scenarios, you can record comprehensive execution traces (including tool logs) prior to user interaction. This enables you to pinpoint and resolve any performance gaps against your release standards. As live traffic begins to flow, you can progressively replace these synthetic setups with real production traces.

A single top-line score will never tell you why an agent failed. To isolate root causes, layer four types of checks: unit tests, deterministic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge rubrics, and integration tests.

Setting up evaluation metrics involves trial and error, and it is tempting to delete a noisy test or swap out sample queries between runs. Every time you add or remove a test, though, you invalidate your baseline: you cannot tell if a score changed because the agent got better or because the test suite moved. Keep your golden dataset frozen while you tune a feature, change one variable at a time, and only retire a test when you are certain the underlying requirement has changed.

LLMs are notoriously inconsistent at grading qualitative 1-to-5 scales; the boundary between a "3" and a "4" is fuzzy and often shifts from run to run.

Whenever possible, turn your judge rubrics into boolean (pass/fail) questions rather than open-ended rating scales. Rather than asking "How helpful was this answer from 1 to 5?", ask "Did the agent verify the account ID before calling the refund tool? (Yes/No)". (We like Adaptive Rubrics)

A response that passes every quality check is still a production failure if it took 40 seconds, made twelve redundant tool calls, and cost sixty cents per query.

Always evaluate the whole system rather than just the final text. Track latency, token consumption (prompt, reasoning, and completion tokens), API cost, and tool-call counts right alongside answer quality.

Switching orchestration frameworks often forces teams to scrap and rewrite their entire testing suite. While most frameworks emit OpenTelemetry traces, they don’t use the same semantic conventions for tool calls, steps, and tokens. Standardizing your traces around open specifications like OpenInference solves this by decoupling evaluation from your orchestration framework. Tools like the agent-eval toolkit support this natively, with minimal setup.

You do not need to build an observability platform from scratch. You can stand up the evaluation pipeline in a couple of hours using open-source utilities like the agent-eval toolkit which pairs OpenTelemetry trace capture with the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). But building your actual evaluation strategy requires more thought.

Start small and stay close to the raw data:

Why the red pen matters: Manually reading twenty traces feels slow compared to auto-generating a chart, but it saves days of wasted effort. Instead of guessing at hypothetical failure modes and writing generic rubrics that miss the mark, an hour with a red pen shows you how your system actually breaks in reality. Aggregating those notes gives you a high-signal list of the exact deterministic assertions and boolean judge checks you need to build next.

Once you have that initial list of checks, tools like agent-eval toolkit takes over the heavy lifting and standardizes execution. Helping you with things like:

Establish a regular cadence of runs with your eval pipeline:

Once your Day 1 framework is active, optimization becomes a disciplined, mechanical loop:

In a real deployment, reaching production quality often takes 50 to 60 small iterations. You are not rewriting the entire system prompt every morning; you are gradually turning individual knobs. One tweak clarifies a tool parameter description. The next adds a negative constraint. The next adjusts the JSON output schema.

For instance, upon completion of each evaluation run, the agent-eval framework constructs an interactive, all-in-one HTML report. This report aligns pass/fail boolean metrics alongside key operational metrics, including latency, token usage, API expenditure, cache utilization, and detailed tool-call execution paths. Additionally, it incorporates automated Gemini root-cause analyses for failed conversational turns, pinpointing the exact prompt directive or parameter setting that triggered the failure to guide your next adjustment.

That data replaces hunches. A prompt change might feel much smarter in manual spot-checking, only for the test battery to reveal that latency doubled and tool-call hallucinations ticked up by 15%.

The lesson for teams building agents: small, measured changes beat big rewrites every time.

Once you trust the signal coming out of your eval pipeline, turn it into a permanent regression safety net:

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Running three or four prompts in a terminal only shows you the final text response. An agent can loop on retries, hallucinate tool parameters, burn token budgets, or trigger duplicate side effects while still returning a polite answer. Automated trace evaluation checks intermediate tool calls, state transitions, latency percentiles, and API cost alongside answer quality so silent regressions don't ship.

Treat evaluation as a progressive rollout from Day 0 to Day 2 rather than an all-or-nothing jump. On Day 0, break your critical user journeys (CUJs) into a handful of test scenarios and run them through ADK user simulations. Read those first 5 to 20 execution traces with a "red pen" to see where the agent hesitates, picks the wrong tool, or rambles.

On Day 1, turn those red-pen notes into automated checks so you can tune one variable at a time. Once your pass rates stabilize, lock those checks into Day 2 CI/CD pull request gates.

LLM judges don't grade 1-to-5 scales consistently because the boundary between a "3" and a "4" shifts from run to run. With Adaptive Rubrics, you split broad quality goals into strict Yes/No questions like "Did the agent verify the account ID before refunding?" Pair those boolean rubrics with deterministic computation metrics for tool parameters and reference checks, plus trace assertions for latency and token budgets.

Yes, if you decouple your evaluation pipeline from your agent framework by standardizing OpenTelemetry traces on OpenInference semantic conventions. The open-source agent-eval toolkit routes those traces straight to the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). You don't have to rewrite grading code when switching orchestration frameworks.

Once your Day 2 pipeline exports traces and scorecards to BigQuery or Cloud Storage, you can compare live traffic against your pre-production baseline. When users hit edge cases your initial scenarios missed, ambient quality agents like The Outer Loop help you diagnose those failing production traces. You can then promote those real traces straight into your Day 0 golden dataset so your test suite evolves with live usage.

Carbon dioxide-spilling converted turbines have been installed in a Mississippi xAI data center despite the known health risks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk's xAI is using eight converted jet engine turbines to power a Mississippi data center. While the generators have the required state-level permission and permits, there remain concerns about the impact they could have on the local environment. The permits allow for a 25% higher limit in pollutants known as volatile organic compounds, but those compounds, paired with the expulsion of carbon monoxide, have environmentalists warning that the use of jet engines to power data centers is a step in the wrong direction. The New York Times reports that the Southern Environmental Law Center has already challenged Mississippi's decision to approve the turbines for use at xAI's data center.

The group argues that xAI has failed to provide guarantees that the jet-powered turbines meet emissions standards for specific pollutants, including formaldehyde. It also accuses xAI of what the report terms the "undercounting" of projected emissions in relation to the data center's power sources. This comes after the same group sued xAI over its use of gas generators without the required permits in an area with more than 100,000 inhabitants.

The generators used by xAI are converted by ProEnergy, a Missouri-based outfit that repurposes the unused General Electric turbines that are typically found attached to Boeing airplanes. The company was quick to turn its attention to the possibility of using the jet engines to power data centers after it was identified that the demand for energy was outpacing the ability of traditional utility companies to keep up. Each turbine is capable of producing up to 50 megawatts of electricity. Normally, that would be enough to keep around 15,000 homes up and running.

The availability of energy has long been an issue for data centers, both existing and those still on the drawing board. A July 2026 report predicted that data centers would use 20% of all U.S. power by 2035, a figure that was a huge 83% increase over the same forecast that was made seven months prior. Despite that news, as of April this year, half of data center buildouts had been canceled or delayed due to, among other things, a lack of power. This is an issue for xAI especially, with Musk saying in August that xAI would increase its data center capacity by 7x before the end of 2027. Delays and cancelations have the potential to derail those plans.

Public sentiment around data centers has turned sour in recent months amid power issues and concerns over how much water these facilities use. While using converted jet engines could potentially help with one issue, it seems unlikely that the pollution they generate will help assuage the concerns of locals anytime soon.
