#10332. The Hidden Problems Behind Website-to-CRM Integrations (and How to Prevent Them)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting a website form to a customer relationship management (CRM) system may seem straightforward. A developer collects information from a form, sends it to an API endpoint, and checks whether a new contact appears in the CRM.

The integration may work perfectly during the first test. However, problems can appear after the website goes live. Some leads may not reach the CRM, duplicate contacts may appear, or important customer information may be missing.

These issues are not always easy to notice. A visitor might see a confirmation message even when the CRM has failed to save their information.

This guide explains common problems in website-to-CRM integrations and practical ways developers can prevent them.

A website-to-CRM integration allows information collected through a website to move into a CRM system. This usually happens through an API or webhook.

For example, when someone completes a contact form, the website collects their name, email address, phone number, and other details. The integration then sends this information to the CRM, where it can be stored and used by the relevant team.

An API integration provides a structured way for two applications to exchange information. Developers who need additional background can also read OpenHerald's overview of API integration.

The process usually involves several steps:

The website validates the submitted information.

The application sends a request to the CRM.

The CRM checks the request and processes the data.

The website or backend handles the response.

Each step needs to work correctly. If one part fails, the information may not reach its destination.

One important rule is to avoid displaying a successful submission message before the application has safely accepted and stored the data.

Data issues are among the most frequent problems in website-to-CRM integrations. They may not appear during development because test submissions usually contain simple, predictable information.

Real visitors enter data in different formats and may submit the same form more than once.

A visitor might complete a contact form and later download a resource using the same email address. If the integration creates a new CRM record every time, the same person may appear several times.

To prevent this, developers should decide how existing contacts will be identified.

Email addresses are commonly used for matching. Before comparing them, the application should remove unnecessary spaces and convert the address to a consistent format.

These values should be treated consistently.

Where supported, an upsert operation can create a new contact or update an existing one instead of creating unnecessary duplicates.

Website forms and CRM systems do not always use the same field structure.

A website may collect a visitor's full name in one field, while the CRM expects separate first-name and last-name values. Other fields may have different names or requirements.

Developers should create a clear mapping between website fields and CRM fields before building the integration.

They should also check which fields are required. If the CRM requires information that the website does not collect, the request may fail.

Different systems may store information in different formats.

For example, one application might use United Kingdom, while another expects UK. Dates, phone numbers, and country values can also create problems.

A reliable integration should validate and standardise these values before sending them to the CRM.

A contact record is more useful when the sales or marketing team knows where the visitor came from.

However, integrations sometimes send only basic contact information and leave out details such as campaign source, landing page, and referral information.

Developers should identify which tracking fields are useful and include them in the integration where appropriate.

Even an integration that works correctly at launch can stop working later.

Authentication credentials may expire, permissions may change, or an API provider may limit the number of requests an application can make.

Three areas deserve particular attention.

Many APIs use access tokens or other credentials to verify requests. Some tokens expire and must be refreshed.

If an application does not handle token renewal correctly, requests may begin failing without an obvious problem on the website.

Developers should implement the authentication process recommended by the CRM provider and monitor failed requests.

An API provider may restrict the number of requests allowed within a particular period.

A marketing campaign can suddenly increase website traffic and generate more form submissions than usual. If the integration exceeds the provider's limit, some requests may be rejected.

The application should recognise rate-limit responses and retry requests according to the provider's instructions.

An integration should not receive more access than it needs.

For example, a form submission service may only need permission to create or update contacts. Giving it full administrative access increases unnecessary security risks.

Use the minimum permissions required for the integration to function.

External services can experience outages, network delays, and temporary errors. If the website sends a form submission directly to the CRM without storing it first, a failed request could mean a lost lead.

A safer approach is to separate receiving a submission from delivering it to the CRM.

Validate the form information on the server.

Store the submission safely in your own system.

Send it to the CRM through a background process.

Retry temporary failures after an appropriate delay.

Flag permanent failures for manual review.

This approach gives the application another opportunity to deliver information when the CRM becomes available again.

A request might reach the CRM successfully, but the response could be lost because of a network problem. The website may then retry the same request, creating a duplicate contact.

Idempotency helps prevent this situation. The application can use a stable identifier for each submission so that repeating the same operation does not create another record.

For example, a combination of the form ID and a normalised email address may help identify repeated submissions. The exact approach depends on the CRM's API capabilities.

This is an illustrative example. Functions such as crmUpsertContact() and scheduleRetry() need to be implemented according to the application and CRM being used.

The important point is that every submission should have a known status: successfully delivered, waiting for another attempt, or requiring manual attention.

A working integration should also protect the quality and security of the information it collects.

Public forms can receive automated submissions from bots. Without appropriate protection, these entries can fill the CRM with unwanted records.

Validation should happen on the server, even if the website already checks information in the browser.

Some forms collect permission to send marketing communications.

If a visitor selects a consent checkbox, that information should also be passed to the CRM. Otherwise, the CRM may contain the contact details without a reliable record of the visitor's choice.

Where relevant, store the consent value, the wording or version displayed, and the time the visitor gave consent.

An integration should only send the information needed for its intended purpose.

Collecting unnecessary personal details increases the amount of information the application must protect and maintain.

Developers should also understand where submitted information is stored, including databases, queues, application logs, and the CRM.

Testing an integration before launch is important, but monitoring it afterwards is just as necessary.

A successful test does not prove that every future submission will work.

The following checks can help identify problems early.

Compare the number of website form submissions with the number of contacts successfully created or updated in the CRM.

A growing difference between the two numbers may indicate that some requests are failing.

Application logs should record the reason a request failed and include a submission identifier where possible.

Avoid storing complete personal details in logs unless there is a specific, justified need.

Developers should configure notifications when the integration's failure rate exceeds an acceptable threshold.

This makes it easier to investigate problems before they affect a large number of submissions.

A scheduled test submission can help confirm that the complete process is still working.

For example, a test lead can be sent periodically and checked to confirm that it reaches the CRM correctly.

Someone should be responsible for monitoring the integration.

This person or team should review errors, check API changes, monitor credentials, and investigate changes to CRM fields.

Without a clear owner, small problems can remain unnoticed for a long time.

Documentation makes integrations easier to maintain and troubleshoot.

It does not need to be complicated. A short reference document covering the main rules can save considerable time when another developer needs to work on the system.

Before deployment, developers should also test unusual situations.

Testing these situations helps reveal weaknesses that ordinary form submissions may not expose.

What is the most common website-to-CRM integration problem?

Duplicate contacts and incorrect field mapping are common problems. They usually happen when the website and CRM handle data differently.

How can developers prevent duplicate contacts?

Normalise email addresses, use a consistent matching rule, and use an upsert operation or idempotency mechanism where the CRM supports it.

Should a website send form data directly to a CRM?

For more reliable handling, the application should first validate and safely store the submission. A background process can then deliver it to the CRM and retry temporary failures.

How can I tell if a CRM integration has stopped working?

Compare website submission counts with CRM records, monitor failed requests, and schedule regular test submissions.

Who should be responsible for maintaining the integration?

Assign a developer or technical team to monitor the connection, review errors, maintain credentials, and respond to API changes.

Developers can make these integrations more reliable by validating information on the server, storing submissions before delivery, using safe retry mechanisms, and monitoring the results after launch.

The goal is simple: every valid submission should reach the CRM, and every failed submission should be visible and recoverable rather than silently lost.

About APK Insight: In this “APK Insight” post, we’ve decompiled the latest version of an application that Google uploaded to the Play Store. When we decompile these files (called APKs, in the case of Android apps), we’re able to see various lines of code within that hint at possible future features. Keep in mind that Google may or may not ever ship these features, and our interpretation of what they are may be imperfect. #10328. Intel 14A moves closer to production as PDK 0.9 becomes available

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Unlock exclusive tools and insights for enthusiasts who want more.

Access Bench, Roadmaps, deep analysis and other exclusive tools.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel Foundry last week made available its Intel 14A process design kit (PDK) version 0.9, an important milestone in the development of this next-generation manufacturing technology. The company says that the production node is reaching performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, and the results are backed by silicon validation. Intel plans to release PDK 1.0 in the first quarter of 2027, which will validate the technology's readiness for high-volume manufacturing (HVM).

A process design kit contains transistor models, design rules, and other information that enables chip designers to develop products for a particular manufacturing process using electronic design automation (EDA) tools. The accuracy and quality of the PDK are crucial for modelling how chips will perform once manufactured, and the release of PDK 0.9 is particularly important because it provides customers with a representation of Intel 14A based on actual silicon measurements rather than on theoretical models or simulations. At this stage, customers can proceed with product development with greater confidence that the technology will meet their requirements when it comes to performance and power consumption.

Intel says that PDK 0.9 lets customers develop designs intended to enter risk production around the time the process is certified, which — provided that PDK accurately reflects the characteristics of manufactured silicon — in many cases enables chip developers to greatly reduce the number of silicon revisions between initial tapeout and mass production. Of course, availability of PDK 0.9 does not indicate that Intel 14A is ready or even close to mass production, as the company expects to validate HVM readiness only with PDK 1.0, sometime in Q1 2027. Again, this does not mean that 14A will enter mass production in early 2027, as Intel itself expects to begin mass production using its 14A process technology in 2028, following risk production for its own products in the second half of 2027.

"From a customer perspective, the PDK 0.9 gives customers the confidence to design products on Intel 14A, as the technology is reaching the industry-standard performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, supported by silicon-backed validation," said Ben Sell, corporate vice president and general manager of front-end technology development at Intel Foundry.

Intel 14A builds on top of architectural foundations introduced with Intel 18A but incorporates several substantial enhancements. These include 2nd Generation RibbonFET 2 gate-all-around (GAA) transistors, PowerDirect backside power delivery (which delivers power right to the transistor source/drain regions without using intermediate connections like TSVs used by PowerVia), and Turbo Cells (high-performance standard cells designed to accelerate timing-critical paths in CPUs and GPUs), which provide chip designers with additional ways to increase performance and optimize power efficiency. Since Intel has previously outlined advantages of these technologies, the latest announcement does not provide updates regarding transistor performance, power consumption, or transistor density figures for Intel 14A.

Beyond improvements to transistor, power delivery, and interconnect technologies, Intel is applying manufacturing experience gained during 12 months of Intel 18A production to accelerate maturation of 14A. Specifically, the company says it has increased investments in inline defect detection, modeling, and simulation to identify potential manufacturing problems earlier in the development cycle. These initiatives are intended to improve manufacturing yields, process variability, ultimately transistor performance, and reduce production cycle times. Intel also says it has introduced several strategic initiatives to accelerate technology development, although it does not quantify their impact.

Another important change concerns Intel's relationships with equipment and materials suppliers. The company says it has revised its collaboration model to involve technology development teams and external suppliers earlier in the process development cycle. The goal of such close collaborations is to address manufacturing defects and improve equipment capabilities before they become obstacles for yield improvements after the technology enters mass production. Intel also conducts regular yield summit meetings with suppliers to accelerate progress on critical development objectives.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

"This has required evolving our collaboration model with equipment and material suppliers, and earlier engagement from our [technology development] leaders with suppliers to drive improvements in defects and equipment capabilities," Sell said.

A bonus of such early collaboration is that equipment and materials suppliers may incorporate Intel's requirements into their standard products and development roadmaps. This could give Intel an advantage over competitors, which would have to either adapt their manufacturing processes to equipment optimized around Intel's requirements or work with suppliers to develop alternative solutions from scratch.

Historically, Intel frequently set the direction for semiconductor manufacturing technologies as other chipmakers subsequently adopted similar approaches. One notable exception was immersion DUV lithography, which Intel adopted after TSMC, which led the development of immersion DUV lithography. By engaging equipment and materials suppliers earlier, Intel could regain some of its historical influence over the development of industry-standard manufacturing solutions, though the importance of this should not be overestimated.

Meanwhile, Intel continues to expand the design ecosystem for its 14A technology. Cadence and Intel have established a design-technology co-optimization (DTCO) collaboration focused on next-generation AI-driven design tools and intellectual property, whereas Keysight, Siemens, and Synopsys have announced design-tool certifications for the process. Intel is also working with other software developers and IP providers to validate design flows and reusable building blocks required by prospective foundry customers.

Intel says it is encouraged by customer feedback regarding the progress of 14A development. However, the company has not identified any customers that have committed to manufacturing products using the technology, disclosed customer tape-outs, or announced specific manufacturing agreements, which is normal for the foundry industry. For Intel Foundry, attracting external customers remains particularly important because development costs of modern leading-edge process technologies can only be recoupled when they are used to make hundreds of millions of chips, way more than Intel sells these days. Meanwhile, the commercial success of Intel Foundry with advanced manufacturing technologies depends not only on transistor performance and manufacturing yields, but also on its ability to deliver predictable development schedules.

Looking ahead, Intel intends to continue improving performance, yields, variability, and manufacturing cycle times of 14A, though for now the company does not disclose any actual goals. Intel also plans additional process enhancements and performance refinements for 14A-E and 14A-P nodes after the initial technology becomes available. However, exact schedules for these nodes are not known at the moment.

"Our next major milestone is the Intel 14A PDK 1.0, expected in the first quarter of 2027," said Ali Farhang, corporate vice president and general manager of Intel Foundry's design technology platform organization. "I am looking forward to sustaining this level of quality across future releases and continuing to earn our customers' trust by delivering on our commitments with a customer-first mindset."

#10326. ‘Cyclon’ uses an old Google Pixel to let AI do literally everything for you – who wants this?

As the AI era continues to find its footing in the consumer market, “Cyclon” is a new smartphone/software experience that, to start, is reusing old Google Pixel phones with a software experience that literally uses AI to do every little thing. And my biggest question is just… who actually wants this?

The pitch of Cyclon, the founders explain in a video on Twitter/X, is to “[build] an AI phone to replace the iPhone.” And the central experience seems to be… talking to a chatbot. Examples in the video include telling the AI to “handle my notifications” and letting it monitor a WhatsApp message and reply to a friend asking for photos by grabbing those photos from your gallery without you as the user needing to touch the device.

Other examples on the company’s website have mixed utility at a glance. “Order my usual on DoorDash” and calling a courier to let them into a building certainly could be useful, but “tell the group chat I’m running ten minutes late” only to have the AI send the message on WhatsApp “in your words” not only seems like a waste of time, but a pointless disconnect from reality (as is all too common with these AI ventures).

The one thing that caught my attention here is building apps on demand and being able to share them. The example given is a language learning app tailored to a specific vacation. It’s a really cool idea, though one I would imagine will have some rocky edges. Google is on a similar track with Gemini Intelligence’s “Create my widget” feature (on that note, when is that releasing on phones Google?), and I’d imagine Gemini will be able to do the same thing in time.

The actual hardware side of things is also… interesting.

Cyclon says that it has a working prototype of the idea running on Google Pixel 8a units, and it’ll ship “first-batch” backers one of those units with their $199 pre-order of a later “Cyclon Phone” that itself is expected to cost $699 – so you’re getting two phones for $699. That alone seems a little questionable in the midst of RAMageddon, but I digress.

That “final” phone hardware… sure is something.

There are some good ideas here, some big promises, but more than anything I just want to know who actually wants to use a phone like this.

I’m all for the idea of AI making my life a little easier (despite finding hardly any ways it can do that so far), but sending everything through a chatbot that does everything in the background and even generates “your words” for responses to my friends and family just sounds awful. And the state of social media isn’t exactly helping matters here. Twitter/X is 90% AI replies for engagement for this sort of thing, and Cyclon is paying influencers to boost that engagement too, so genuine reactions just seem impossible to find.

Sound off in the comments below if you’re an actual human with an opinion on this one, because I’m dying to know what real people think of this.

#10323. Three layers, one repo: a tour of google/adk-recipes

A developer building on Google Cloud today can scaffold a long-horizon memory agent from the terminal, install a retail vertical skill into a coding assistant with one line, and deploy a fully-wired production service to Cloud Run, Agent Runtime, Agent Garden, or Gemini Enterprise app — all from the same curated source tree. That tree is google/adk-recipes, the restructured successor to google/adk-samples that now serves Agent Platform from a single source of truth.

ADK recipes are agent-friendly: we've made it easy for your agents to 1) discover relevant recipes for various agent use cases, and 2) install and use them automatically from your favorite harness.

google/adk-recipes splits its content into three top-level folders: core/ for reference patterns curated by the Agents CLI and DevRel teams, plugins/ for installable vertical skills packaged as Plugin Recipes conforming to the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 spec, and contrib/ for end-to-end deployable services across five languages. Every piece has been designed with machine readability as the primary consideration.

What follows is a walkthrough of the repository's shape, three worked examples (one per layer), and a reflection on what the new structure enables.

Three top-level roots, each anchored on one reader action:

Every recipe ships a validated manifest.yaml, an AGENTS.md that tells the coding assistant opening it what the recipe is and how to use it, and (for contrib/ and plugins/) a Dockerfile plus a runnability test. CI enforces the standards on every PR, and a suite of coding-assistant skills in the repo (prepare-python-recipe, generate-manifest, github-pr-review, and others) does most of the preparation, so your agent can scaffold, validate, and submit a recipe end-to-end.

Every AGENTS.md, every manifest.yaml, every SKILL.md is written primarily for the AI coding assistant that will open the repo on your behalf; README.md files are what humans still read. The handbook glossary captures the dual framing: "Recipe — a runnable agent example (or importable agent module) under core/, contrib/, or plugins/, consumed by ADK developers and coding agents alike."

adk-recipes serves as the single source of truth for ADK content across the three Agent Platform surfaces that consume it. Each reads the repository through its own protocol.

Agents CLI reads core/. When a developer runs uvx --from google-agents-cli agents-cli create my-agent, Agents CLI can scaffold on demand a core/ recipe. The CLI, launched earlier this year at Google Cloud Next, exposes the full scaffold → deploy → evaluate → publish lifecycle; the base content it serves comes from our recipes. Horizon, the reference harness we examine below, is an example pattern Agents CLI treats as a production template.

Agent Garden features a curated selection. The developer-facing path into the repository is a pull request to contrib/: a recipe lands in GitHub, becomes part of the public ADK ecosystem, and from that moment is discoverable by other developers and by coding assistants reading the repository. Appearing in the Agent Garden gallery is a separate editorial decision made by the Agent Garden team, which curates a subset of recipes and promotes them to the public catalog on their own cadence. Once an agent is in the gallery, any developer in a Google Cloud project gets a one-click install.

Beyond these primary surfaces, Google Cloud's own infrastructure components appear as building blocks inside the recipes themselves. Horizon uses Agent Platform Sandboxes for per-user sandboxing, Memory Bank for durable memory, and Agent Platform Sessions for session state. The retail product-search Plugin Recipe uses Vector Search 2.0 on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform paired with BigQuery. The retail virtual-tryon Plugin Recipe uses Gemini image models for still composites and Veo 3.1 Reference-to-Video for animated outputs. The recipes do not just live alongside Agent Platform; they document it in use.

For developers working with coding assistants outside Google Cloud's tooling (Claude Code, Antigravity, Cursor, Windsurf, Zed, and others), Plugin Recipes additionally install through the open npx skills CLI maintained by vercel-labs.

Building an agent that can handle a single conversation is a well-understood problem. Building one that holds its own across days of interaction, remembering what the user did yesterday and planning tasks for tomorrow, is a materially different undertaking. The hard parts of such a long-horizon system are the same regardless of what the agent is being built to do: cross-session memory, per-user sandboxing of tool execution, secrets management, guardrails against runaway behaviour, and the orchestration primitives that make multi-step tasks resumable. Horizon, which lives at core/python/long-horizon-harness, is the reference harness we built to collect these primitives in one place.

The shortest path from a fresh clone to a running Horizon instance:

make deploy rolls the backend to Cloud Run through Terraform; make destroy tears it back down. Horizon's evaluation suite lives in tests/eval/evalsets/ as eighteen named behavioural rubrics covering memory recall, injection-based exfiltration attempts, guardrail halting under adversarial input, tool-selection discipline, and a journey-level plot-artifact evaluation. The rubrics are executed with uv run pytest tests/eval.

A first chat turn against the running backend confirms the memory layer is wired correctly:

Horizon is the harness we give coding assistants to study before building something harder. When a developer asks their assistant to build an agent that must run across days of interaction, the right next step is uvx google-agents-cli create with Horizon as the scaffold, and the rest of the harness falls into place.

Horizon encodes a reference pattern. It does not encode a vertical. For that, the second layer of the repository holds Plugin Recipes, each addressing a specific industry domain. The first vertical fully covered in this launch is retail, available under plugins/retail/.

The Plugin Recipe format sits on top of the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 specification published at agent-plugins.org, an open standard for packaging installable AI skills across coding assistants. The specification defines a plugin as a bundle of one or more skills with a top-level plugin.json manifest declaring name, version, license, and ownership. Each skill is a self-contained folder with a SKILL.md conversational installer, a README.md, an EVAL.yaml of quality gates, and the scripts and assets the skill needs to do its work. The retail plugin's layout is:

Two skills ship in this plugin today. The first, retail-product-search, builds an ADK agent with a vector-search retrieval tool backed by Vector Search 2.0 on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform and a BigQuery catalog. The skill handles the entire pipeline: ingesting a CSV or JSON catalog into BigQuery, generating multimodal and text embeddings with Vertex AI Embeddings, building the Vector Search collection, scaffolding the ADK agent with a retrieve_docs tool, and deploying to Cloud Run. The agent is deliberately single-agent: one root_agent with one retrieval tool and no coordinator layer. The semantic complexity sits in the retrieval pipeline and the evaluator rubric, not in agent-to-agent communication; adding orchestration layers would obscure the pattern rather than clarify it.

The second skill, retail-virtual-tryon, generates a photorealistic composite of a model wearing or holding a product, with an optional eight-second studio catwalk video on top. It uses gemini-2.5-flash-image by default, falling back to gemini-2.5-pro-image when editorial quality matters more than latency. For video it uses veo-3.1-generate-001 in Reference-to-Video mode, conditioning the generated motion on the still produced by the image pipeline. The skill ships a pre-flight product-cutout classifier and configurable safety levels, operational details that become obvious after the third real production deployment.

Both skills install into any coding assistant that reads skills with a single command:

The --full-depth flag instructs the CLI to walk past its default top-level lookup and discover the nested plugins/retail/skills/ structure. The install lands in ~/.agents/skills// and is symlinked into every detected coding assistant on the local machine:

The skill ecosystem is designed to compose. The retail-product-search SKILL.md instructs the host coding assistant to pull two companion skills from google/skills if they are not already installed, keeping cross-cutting concerns like BigQuery basics and Gemini API usage in one place and reusable by multiple vertical plugins.

Once installed, the coding assistant reads SKILL.md and initiates a Q-MODE interview. For retail-product-search, Quick Start is two questions (GCP project ID and the path to product data, with sensible defaults for both); Full Setup adds two more covering product-fields level and GCP region. By the end of the interview the skill has written a ./design-spec.md reflecting the project's actual shape and executed scripts/setup.py to land the catalog in BigQuery, generate the embeddings, build the Vector Search collection, and run EVAL.yaml against the result. If the rubric and assertion checks fall below the 80 percent passing target, the skill halts and surfaces the specific failures.

An end-to-end setup run on the bundled five-product demo catalog produces:

A live retrieval against the collection the skill has just provisioned returns:

The virtual-tryon skill is faster to demonstrate interactively. A direct call to generate_tryon_image with a user portrait and a product image produces the photorealistic composite shown at the top of this section in roughly ten seconds. The same inputs piped through generate_tryon_video and Veo Reference-to-Video turn that still into an eight-second studio catwalk animation of the composite in motion.

The pipeline is category-agnostic. Swapping the product image for a pair of sunglasses, with product_category='eyewear', invokes the same generate_tryon_image call and produces a composite in the correct category without any further configuration:

Retail is the first vertical fully covered in this format. Additional verticals will land as their own folders under plugins/, following the same anatomy and the same Agent Plugins specification, so developers can expect a consistent install experience across industries.

The contrib/ layer is where ADK recipes become end-to-end deployable services. Twelve Python agents live there today, spanning the kinds of applications ADK is typically used to build in practice: finance (financial advisor, small-business loan processor), evaluation (LLM auditor), document and knowledge pipelines (high-volume document analyzer, multiformat hybrid RAG), content and brand (brand-aligned presentations, brand-search optimization, on-brand genmedia), retail (product search, virtual try-on), and framework examples (agent-skills tutorial, staged UI widgets). Java, Go, TypeScript, and Kotlin each carry smaller sets. Every entry in contrib/ is a worked example of how a core/ pattern or a plugins/ skill becomes a running service.

To make the relationship between a plugin and its deployable counterpart concrete, take contrib/python/retail-product-search as the example. The retail plugin from the previous section walked a developer through a conversational install that provisioned the Vector Search collection, the BigQuery catalog, and the embeddings. The contrib agent is the deployable counterpart: a long-running service that reads from that collection and answers natural-language shopping queries over it. The plugin owns the one-shot setup; the contrib agent owns the service shape. Both are maintained by the same team to stay synchronised, and the agent's README directs new users to run the plugin's setup first:

Once the collection (retail-skill-products-collection) is populated, the agent is cloned out of contrib/python/retail-product-search, pointed at a GCP project, and surfaced on one of five deployment personas controlled by boolean flags in .env.

Once the deployment is live, the agent exposes a hosted shopping-assistant endpoint. The same retrieve_docs tool we called directly from Python now sits behind a chat interface, reachable through the deployed Reasoning Engine or, during development, through the ADK Web UI. The exact same agent code answering the exact same shopping query, run locally for inspection:

The agent graph on the left of the UI confirms the single-agent architecture (product_search_agent → retrieve_docs). The right panel traces each tool invocation. The response grounds in a real catalog row from Vector Search: "Sony Wireless Headphones · Price: $199.99 · Brand: Sony · Rating: 4.5/5 (50 in stock) · Premium noise-cancelling over-ear headphones featuring an impressive 30-hour battery life." No products are hallucinated; the Vector Search collection on top of BigQuery is the source of truth.

That is what the retail plugin gives a team. The plugin encodes the data-pipeline setup once, through a conversational install any coding assistant can execute. The contrib agent is the running service, deployable in five shapes, that any organisation can take as a starting point and swap in its own catalog. The virtual-tryon counterpart in contrib/python/retail-virtual-tryon follows the same pattern: a smaller root agent that orchestrates try_on_product_image and try_on_product_video tools, deployable in the same five shapes, built on the virtual-tryon plugin's one-shot setup.

A reference harness without a vertical is a toy; a vertical skill without a deployable artifact is a slide; a deployable agent without a reference pattern is a prototype no one can repeat. Shipping all three in one repository, under a shared manifest schema and governance pipeline, lets a developer or a coding assistant walk the stack in either direction, with every Agent Platform surface reading from the same source of truth.

The practical result is a lower barrier from "I need an agent that does X" to a running production service: a coding assistant can scaffold a Horizon-shaped agent, install a vertical Plugin Recipe, and reach the deployment transcripts in contrib/ without translating between repositories or reconciling manifest formats. For teams building vertical solutions, Plugin Recipes package domain expertise once and distribute it to every coding assistant on the market with the same install flow and quality gates.

Not an officially supported Google product. Recipes are for demonstration and as starting points, not production use.

#10322. Top 5 AI Animation Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, animating a ten-second scene meant storyboards, rigging and a lot of patience. Today you can type a sentence, upload a drawing or point at a reference clip and get moving footage back in a minute or two. The results are uneven, but they are good enough that designers, marketers and indie developers now use them for storyboards, product teasers, game trailers and social content.

The catch is that these tools are not interchangeable. Some excel at cinematic camera moves, some at editing footage you already have, and some at a particular visual style. This guide compares five AI animation generators, what each one is best at and where it falls short. Plans, credits and model names change quickly, so check each vendor's pricing page before you commit.

Runway is a cloud workspace for generating and editing video, images and audio in one place. Its current video model is Gen-4.5, which Runway promotes for motion quality, prompt adherence and visual fidelity. The platform also offers agent and workflow tools, mobile apps for iPhone and Android, and an API for developers.

Best for: Creators who want to generate a shot and then keep refining it in the same environment, and teams that need an API.

Limitations: There is a lot to learn, and credit costs add up when you iterate. Runway offers a free trial, but check the pricing page for current limits.

Kling AI is an AI video and image generator with a creative studio for image generation, video generation and motion control. It also includes an agent for brainstorming and scripting, and a node-based canvas for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video with multiple rounds of editing. Kling lists advertising, film, music videos and e-commerce among its use cases, and offers an API platform and automation options for producing video at scale.

Best for: Marketers and studios that want to move from script to storyboard to footage in a single workspace.

Limitations: The feature set is broad, so newcomers may need time to find the right workflow. Free credits were not described on the pages I checked, so confirm what is available before relying on it.

Luma focuses on turning still images into video with camera motion. Its workspace can create transitions between frames, extend clips, transform footage into new styles, reframe aspect ratios and add voice, music, captions and lip sync. It runs Luma's own Ray models and also gives access to several third-party models, so you can compare results without switching platforms.

Best for: Designers and illustrators who already have key art, product shots or concept images and want to bring them to life.

Limitations: Paid plans start at around $30 a month at the time of writing, and generation credits can run out quickly if you test many variations.

Pika is a creative platform covering video, images and audio. Its Video Studio can produce anything from a short clip to a multi-shot video of around 30 seconds, and its toolkit includes Extend Video, Motion Transfer, Swap Anything and Color Grade. Character Studio lets you create reusable characters, and separate studios handle music, speech and sound effects. Pika also exposes several video models, including its own Pika 2.5 alongside third-party options.

Best for: Social content creators who want effects, sound and edits in one tool.

Limitations: With so many tools and models, results vary from one to the next. Check the current pricing page for credit details.

Pollo AI is a generator that leans into one look: anime. Its animation generator creates anime-style videos from a text prompt and lets you adjust the video content, output style and size. The page offers eleven Japanese animator-inspired style options, such as Hayao Miyazaki and Makoto Shinkai, and the platform also provides a separate tool that converts existing video into anime.

Best for: Creators who want a consistent anime look for shorts, fan projects, music visuals or social posts without learning a full editing suite.

Limitations: The generator page describes text prompts as its input and does not state clip length, resolution or credit details, so test a few generations and review the terms before planning a project around it. As with any style-focused tool, the look is distinctive, which is a strength for anime projects and a limit for anything else.

Start with the job rather than the brand. If you need to edit and refine footage, Runway is a natural fit. If you want a script-to-video pipeline, look at Kling. If you have strong still images, try Luma. If you want effects and sound for social posts, Pika covers a lot of ground. If anime is the goal, Pollo AI is built for it.

Run the same prompt through two or three tools before paying for anything. Differences in motion, faces and text rendering show up quickly, and they are often more important than the feature list.

AI animation tools have moved from novelty to practical production aid. None of them replaces a skilled animator, but each can speed up storyboards, concepts and short-form content when matched to the right job. Try a few with a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final cut. If you want a quick way to experiment with anime styles, you can create anime clips from a prompt and see how the results compare with the other tools on this list.

Are AI animation generators free? Most offer a free trial or limited credits, with paid plans for more generations and higher quality. Terms change often, so read each pricing page.

Can I use AI-generated animation commercially? It depends on the provider's terms and your plan. Check the licensing section before publishing client work.

Do I need animation experience? No, but knowing basic shot composition helps you write better prompts and judge the results.

Which tool is best for beginners? Pick one with a simple prompt-first workflow, test the free tier and move to a more advanced tool as your needs grow.

#10320. OpenAI introduces program to reduce AI data center energy demand during peak hours

This move could help data centers operate sooner without waiting for the rest of the grid to catch up to its peak demand.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Electricity consumption is one of the biggest concerns that residents bring up whenever a data center is proposed in their community, which is why OpenAI is taking steps to minimize its impact on the local electricity grid. According to the company, its Grid Protectors Program aims to reduce the power consumption of its data centers as local demand increases, ensuring that the grid is not overwhelmed during peak hours. Aside from ensuring that the lights remain on for everyone, it would also allow its data centers to connect to the grid and go online sooner instead of waiting for the additional power sources to come online.

“Whether it’s heating homes during a northern cold snap or running air conditioning through a southern heat wave, families need reliable electricity. Our goal is to make campus flexibility a resource that utilities can call on when communities need it most,” OpenAI said in its blog. It also added, “Through Grid Protectors, our ambition is to make this kind of flexibility a core part of how we build and operate future campuses. That means designing facilities to reduce electricity use when needed and working with utilities to agree on how much demand can be reduced, how quickly and for how long. The arrangements will differ by location, and we will share additional commitments as they are finalized.”

OpenAI is testing the program in its planned Effingham County campus in Georgia, with the company saying that it has already signed a 3.2-gigawatt agreement with Georgia Power that included 1,000 megawatts of flexible demand response. This ensures that if demand jumps on the hottest days of the year, there would be enough power to accommodate the hundreds of thousands of air conditioners keeping Georgians cool. With the average American home drawing around 1,200 kilowatts, the 1,000-megawatt buffer is more than enough to power around 800,000 households — nearly 20% of the state’s homes.

This technology, called demand response, has been around for decades now, where large operators reduce their demand in response to a peak in demand from other consumers. Before automation, grid operators would manually call up industrial customers like steel mills and ask them to cut consumption to ensure the reliability of the entire grid. But with modern technologies and IoT, it can now be done automatically, allowing data centers to reduce their load in under a minute. In fact, Nvidia has already been experimenting with a similar system in the UK and has proven that it works.

#10318. Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

#10317. ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

Microsoft's claimed performance gains will need to be tested

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following a resurgence of 8GB RAM laptops earlier this year, and in the wake of promises to improve Windows 11 performance using just 8GB of RAM, Microsoft has today announced what it claims are a series of performance improvements. Driven by an ongoing global PC component shortage that is forcing manufacturers to dumb down specs, the company says that with 8GB of RAM, Windows is now 20% more responsive and 40% faster when it comes to multitasking.

The development shared today by Microsoft is an update to a commitment earlier this year to improve the quality and performance of Windows 11, a major bugbear of users since its launch. Microsoft says it has taken a three-pronged approach to memory optimization: making better use of memory in Windows 11 PCs, protecting interactions that need to feel instant, and ensuring key apps and Windows use less memory. While the company admits you'll be able to do more on 16GB or 32GB of RAM, it says "we're making sure you can do more, even with 8GB."

The headline performance gain is a claim that Windows is now up to 20% more responsive on 8GB PCs. Likewise, multitasking is now purportedly 40% faster in Microsoft's lab tests. Of course, these are internal tests conducted in favorable conditions by Microsoft.

Microsoft says it has tuned compression for useful apps and data, and can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory, rather than moving it to storage. In practice, this should mean that apps and tabs left open should be less likely to reload when you go back to them.

The claim of 20% more responsiveness specifically relates to tasks like opening Start, searching, right-clicking, opening Settings, using the Snipping Tool, and opening File Explorer. The company says it has achieved this by backing off background tasks like indexing while you're using your PC actively. Edge now reportedly opens up to 33% faster. There's also an Efficiency Mode for Microsoft Teams, which can be set to on by default, improving launch times by 20%.

Microsoft says that its claims of 40% speed boosts to multitasking are based on a demanding workflow of 20 Microsoft Edge Tabs, a 4K video, large Word, PowerPoint, and Excel files, Outlook, and File Explorer all being opened one after the other.

Other improvements include reducing committed-memory growth by up to 900 MB in apps like Microsoft Edge and 365 apps, and reducing the memory footprint of Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook by 360MB. Third-party apps can also benefit from improved memory management, with Microsoft stating WhatsApp is already taking advantage.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Moving forward, Microsoft says it has plans to make memory compression even smarter and more efficient. Windows features and apps will share common parts, such as Search and Widgets using one web Engine, for instance. WinUI 3 apps like File Explorer and Notepad will be designed to use less memory and open faster, and Microsoft is working with PC makers and silicon manufacturers to reduce how much memory a PC needs to hold in reserve to support its hardware, making more space for Windows.
