#10265. Integrating TorchTPU into TorchTitan and Optimizing Models for TPU

Over the past years, Meta’s TorchTitan has emerged as the PyTorch ecosystem’s premier clean-room reference platform for large-scale generative AI model training. Built entirely on PyTorch-native scaling primitives, TorchTitan provides an unencumbered, highly modular showcase for pre-training flagship dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures like Llama 3, Qwen 3, and FLUX.

Historically, developers looking to train PyTorch models on Google Cloud TPUs faced some challenges: either undertake a complete codebase rewrite into JAX, or grapple with legacy torch_xla. That paradigm has officially shifted. With the launch of TorchTPU, Cloud TPUs now feature a native backend (torch.device("tpu")) integrated directly into PyTorch's core interface. Enabling a training script to use TPUs now only requires minimal changes to your device initialization code. Providing device compatibility, however, is only half the battle, developers also need high performance production-grade, distributed training recipes. This is where TorchTitan on TPU comes in. TorchTitan TPU is a fork of Meta’s TorchTitan implementation that is adapted to enable model training on Google Cloud TPUs. The fork is available at https://github.com/google-pytorch/torchtitan/

Rather than requiring engineering teams to invent custom TPU sharding strategies or hand-craft low-level XLA communication passes from scratch, Google and Meta co-developed TPU-native execution passes directly inside TorchTitan. TorchTitan serves as the definitive vehicle for scaling PyTorch on Google Cloud TPUs.

TorchTitan provides official, battle-tested recipes for pre-training large dense models (such as Llama 3 70B/405B and Qwen 3 8B/32B), Mixture-of-Experts (such as Qwen 3 235B MoE), and diffusion transformers (such as FLUX.1 12B).

Beyond initial pre-training, TorchTitan serves as the scalable trainer inside post-training pipelines like VeRL for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) alignment. In these workflows, TorchTitan handles the parameter update step while synchronizing weights with high-throughput serving engines like vLLM-TorchTPU.

Parallelism is also kept separate from the model code: techniques such as fully sharded data parallelism and tensor parallelism are layered onto a parallelism-agnostic model implementation. The exception is expert parallelism: the MoE layer performs explicit communication through a pluggable token dispatcher that each backend can implement in its own way.

TorchTitan builds a model from a configuration tree, and that tree can be rewritten before the model is constructed. This offers a clean way to replace individual blocks of a model with versions that run better on TPU, without touching the model definition or the shared trainer loop.

We use this actively. The TPU recipes replace standard attention with Splash Attention, a Pallas kernel that handles causal, sliding-window and packed-document masks without materializing the attention matrix; replace the final projection and cross-entropy loss with a fused Pallas kernel that never writes the full logits tensor to HBM; and replace MoE expert matmuls with grouped Pallas kernels. Each kernel is a JAX function turned into a PyTorch custom op by TorchTPU's torch.tpu.pallas.jax_op decorator, which is all the glue a new kernel needs. Each replacement is a config flag, so a recipe can turn it on, and a test can compare it against the plain PyTorch path it replaces. Kernels can come from tokamax, the OpenXLA kernel library for TPU and GPU.

FSDP2 is built around the GPU execution model. Its all-gathers and reduce-scatters are issued from module hooks as explicit collective calls, and overlap with compute comes from running them on dedicated CUDA streams synchronized with events. None of that is visible to a compiler: the collectives sit outside the traced region, so on TPU a torch.compile region breaks at every FSDP hook and a whole-step graph cannot be captured at all. TorchTPU provides no-op stream and event objects only so that FSDP2 runs; XLA has no use for them, since it schedules work from data dependencies.

SimpleFSDP takes the compiler's view instead. Parameter sharding is a DTensor parametrization: reading a module's weight performs a redistribute from sharded to replicated, which is the all-gather, and its autograd backward is the reduce-scatter. There are no hooks and no explicit collective calls, so both collectives are ordinary nodes in the traced graph, whether that graph is a per-block torch.compile region or the Graph Trainer's single make_fx step. The XLA compiler schedules the all-gathers ahead of the matmuls that need them and overlaps the reduce-scatters with the backward compute, inside one program. Mixed precision is part of the same mechanism, a policy that sets the gather dtype (bf16 parameters) and the reduce dtype (fp32 or bf16 gradients) on that redistribute. Hybrid sharding for multislice is the same code on a 2-D mesh: shard within a slice over ICI, replicate across slices over DCN.

On FLUX this is what brought TorchTitan to 99% performance parity with JAX MaxDiffusion: the per-layer HLO for a SimpleFSDP block is a single module with the weight all-gathers, matmuls, attention and norms fused together, which is the shape of program XLA was built to optimize.

Many PyTorch trainers either run in eager mode with CUDA graphs to cut launch overhead, or apply torch.compile to isolated regions, typically one transformer block at a time. TorchTPU does not yet support the PyTorch torch.Stream abstraction, which limits the performance of mixing eager-based FSDP2 with layer-wise torch.compile. SimpleFSDP helps by expressing parameter sharding in a way that is compatible with compilation, whether using layer-wise torch.compile or full-graph capture.

TorchTitan's Graph Trainer provides full training-step capture (forward, loss and backward), producing one FX graph ahead of time with PyTorch's make_fx tracer, skipping AOTAutograd partitioning, so the whole computation is visible to XLA). GraphTrainer also runs optimization passes: to implement graph-based activation memory policy that decides what to save, recompute or offload. The train step then executes as a single XLA program.

This is the model XLA was built for, and it makes PyTorch training on TPU conceptually the same as JAX, where the whole train step is one jitted function. Owning the whole step as one graph is also what makes the large-model compilation techniques possible: scan over layers, which runs the decoder layers as a single loop body so that compile time and memory stop growing with depth, and the graph splitter, which cuts the step into separately compiled forward and backward programs with activations offloaded to host memory in between. On Qwen3 235B this path reaches 29%+ MFU.

The optimizer can join the graph as well: While the fused_adam optimizer is very performant, a functional AdamW update can be traced into the train-step (functional_adamw_step) and ensures an iteration has no host-side work between the first forward op and the weight write-back. On Qwen3-8B this path reaches 51-52% MFU.

Every lever below is described in the TorchTitan TPU optimization guide, and nearly all of them are already wired into the recipes. For most models, getting a good MFU on TPU is a sequence of configuration changes on an existing recipe rather than new code. The order matters, because each step changes what the next one measures. A lot of optimizations in TorchTitan are available as ready-to-use building blocks that can be enabled by a flag flip (see illustration below).

4. Tune Compiler, Communication and Profile

To check that TorchTitan runs efficiently across different classes of models, we tuned and scaled several of them on TPU v7x (Ironwood). At scale, Llama 3 405B (large dense model) holds about 57% MFU from 128 to 1,024 chips. Qwen3 235B (large MoE model) reaches 32.42% MFU on a single 1,024-chip slice and 30.51% on 8,192 chips across eight slices. Muse Glimmer 30B (modern dense model) reaches 43.4% MFU on 128 chips (4x4x8). FLUX.1 (diffusion model) reaches 19.4% MFU on 1,024 chips (8x8x16).

The following case studies show how we got there for three of these models, each representing a different class:

FLUX.1 is a 12-billion-parameter diffusion transformer, which makes it a useful check that the playbook above is not specific to LLMs. We trained it at 1024x1024 resolution on a TPU v6e-8 with FSDP in bf16 and a batch of three samples. Out of the box, in eager mode with FSDP2 and full activation checkpointing, a step took 11.49 seconds. Applying the playbook in order brought that down one stage at a time. Deferred-and-fused eager execution cut the number of HLO module invocations by 28% and the step to 9.95 s (15.5% improvement). Replacing FSDP2 with SimpleFSDP reduced the step to 6.52 s (52.6% improvement): the trace was dominated by FSDP2 copying parameter shards into a flat staging buffer before each all-gather and back out into per-parameter tensors afterwards; SimpleFSDP all-gathers each parameter directly, so the staging buffer and both copies are gone (see the SimpleFSDP section above). The splash attention kernel, with sequence packing and segment ids so that no MXU cycles go to padding, took the step to 5.55 s (17.5% improvement), and per-block torch.compile, which hands XLA large sub-graphs instead of fine-grained modules and moves control flow out of the Python interpreter, took it to 3.02 s (83.8% improvement).

The final step came from three small, local rewrites in the model code that the profile pointed at: splitting a fused projection into two separate linear layers so its output no longer had to be sliced apart, simplifying the rotary embedding to plain element-wise math, and unpacking Q, K and V with a view instead of a transpose. None of them changes what the model computes; each removes copies and lets XLA fuse more of the graph. Together they brought the step to 2.78 s (8.6% improvement), 4.1x faster than the starting point and about 99% of the throughput of the JAX-based MaxDiffusion on the same hardware.

We also scaled FLUX from 8 to 1,024 v7x chips, keeping three images per TPU. The best configuration went from 2.53 s per step (20.76% MFU) on 8 chips to 2.71 s (19.4% MFU) on 1,024, which is 93% weak-scaling efficiency. Past one 64-chip cube, pure FSDP slows down due to the model’s compact size. Hybrid sharding via HSDP fixes this: it shards within each 4x4x4 cube and replicates across cubes. We used the topology-aware device mesh, which maps the parallelism optimally to the physical 8x8x16 torus.

Muse Glimmer 30B is a modern dense decoder: 52 layers, grouped-query attention with 32 query and 2 key-value heads, a 202k-token vocabulary with a soft-capped output head, and a layer pattern that mixes sliding-window and global attention, with RoPE skipped on every fourth layer. It is the case that shows how much works out of the box. The model definition, the parallelization and the training loop ran on TPU unchanged; the port came down to two model-specific pieces. Upstream implements the sliding-window pattern with FlexAttention, which has no TPU backend, so the first step was a windowed SDPA inner attention that reproduces the window exactly and gives a correctness baseline, checked against upstream on CPU. The second piece was the fused cross-entropy kernel, which we extended to apply the soft-cap to each vocabulary tile as it computes the loss, so the 202k-wide logits never have to be materialized; without that the LM head alone dominated memory. Everything else, including the Graph Trainer wrapper, was the standard onboarding ladder, with each feature behind a flag and its own test. The resulting recipe trains the 30B on 64 v7x chips (a 4x4x4 slice) at sequence length 16,384 with one sequence per device, Graph Trainer plus SimpleFSDP and fp32 gradient reductions, and reaches 5,848 tokens per second per chip at ~44% MFU.

Qwen3-235B-A22B is the largest MOE model in this set: 94 layers, 128 experts with 8 active per token, 235 billion parameters of which 22 billion are active per token. It is the case that exercises the large-model half of the playbook. At this depth compiling the whole unrolled step as one program is not an option: XLA plans every buffer of a program at compile time, and a 94-layer graph laid out flat asks for more HBM than the chip has before a step can run, while its compile time grows with every layer. Getting it to work at all meant enabling one of the two Graph Trainer paths for large models. Scan over layers is the XLA-native answer, the same construct JAX and MaxText rely on: the 94 identical layers become one compiled loop body, so compile time and program size stop growing with depth. The graph splitter is the more flexible alternative: it keeps the layers unrolled but cuts the captured step into per-layer executables, which works no matter what kind of layers you have (and does not require layers to be the same). We run both for this model and they land within a few percent of each other. Either way the activations do not fit in HBM at sequence length 4096 with 16 sequences per device, so both paths offload layer activations to host memory and prefetch them back for the backward pass.

From there the playbook applied, one change at a time, each measured on the same 64-chip slice. We raised the scoped VMEM budget so the compiler could keep more kernel intermediates on chip, which also removed an exposed update at the tail of the optimizer step. We enabled a set of libtpu scheduling flags that let XLA's latency-hiding scheduler overlap the FSDP all-gathers and reduce-scatters with the surrounding compute and offload the reduce-scatters to the SparseCores, so most of the collective time is hidden behind compute, leaving only a small exposed reduce-scatter tail in the backward pass. We switched attention to the tokamax splash kernel, and replaced the expert matmuls with specialized paired grouped-matmul kernels backed by a K-tiled weight-gradient kernel, with hierarchical reduce-scatter and the SparseCore latency-hiding scheduler to hide the expert-gradient collectives. A fused route-score backward removed a large fp32 copy from the MoE backward pass. The last round of fixes came from profiling at 1,024 chips: keeping the blocked activation layout under scan offload, raising the backward layer-scheduler threshold so the expert reduce-scatters overlap with compute again, and pipelining small reduce-scatters across the scan loop's back-edge. The recipe runs at ~32% MFU on 256 v7x chips with fp32 gradient reductions kept throughout.

We also used this model to test how far TorchTPU scales, from 64 chips up to 8,192-chip v7x pod scale, in both single-slice and multi-slice configurations. A TPU slice is a set of chips connected directly over the inter-chip interconnect (ICI); multislice lets one training job span several slices connected over the data-center network (DCN), which is how a job grows past the largest single slice. Within a slice we run FSDP over ICI, sharding parameters, gradients and optimizer state across the slice's chips; across slices the model is replicated and the gradients are all-reduced over DCN once per step, so the only traffic on the slower network is a single gradient reduction, which overlaps with the backward pass. That is hybrid sharded data parallelism (HSDP), and it is what the 2,048- to 8,192-chip runs use, with 1,024-chip slices as the sharding group. The framework behaved as expected throughout: per-chip throughput dropped by 8% going from 64 to 8,192 chips (4,568 to 4,208 tokens per second per chip), near-linear scaling across 128x more chips. One caveat: for a model of this size, data parallelism beyond about 1K chips is a scaling experiment rather than a training recipe. This is a weak-scaling run: the batch per device stays fixed as chips are added, so the global batch grows with the chip count and at 8k chips is larger than one would use to train this model. What the run shows is that the framework, the compiler and the collectives hold up at that scale, not that this is the configuration to train with.

The three case studies above are deep dives: one model at a time, with a parity test and a profile behind every change. A team arriving on TPU usually has a different problem. They have a model they already train, often a Hugging Face one, and quite likely it won’t be the model that TorchTitan ships for TPU. They may well want every last percent out of it eventually, but first they need an easy way to get it running at all. That is the problem the Transformers modeling backend solves: it builds the model from the Transformers library by its Hub id and runs it through TorchTitan's trainer and parallelism instead of its own. On TPU the backend has a TorchTPU lane with a single configuration that applies the first rungs of the playbook optimizations to any supported checkpoint, with no change to the model code: SimpleFSDP in place of FSDP2, per-block torch.compile, the splash attention kernel registered as an attention implementation with the transformers attention interface so the model's own attention calls land on it, the fused Pallas cross entropy fed the final hidden states and the head weight instead of materialized logits, and, for mixture-of-experts checkpoints, the TPU grouped-matmul experts substituted for the portable implementation. Most of the dense architectures the backend supports train this way unmodified; mixture-of-experts checkpoints run through the same path but without expert parallelism yet. None of this reaches the hand-tuned numbers above, but it is a working, reasonably efficient first run for a model that has no TPU recipe yet, and a way to find out whether it deserves one.

Sooner or later a model deserves more than a generic path: it is the one you will train for months, it has an architecture the backend cannot express, or the out-of-the-box number is not the one you need. Porting a model to a new accelerator used to be a specialist project measured in weeks, with the hard-won lessons living in one engineer's head. On TorchTitan TPU it is a repeatable procedure. The repository ships a model-onboarding skill for coding agents that captures how the case studies above were done and walks an engineer, or the agent itself, through the same ladder, with the tests, the acceptance bars and the traps built in.

The roadmap runs along five lines: production readiness, precision, hardware, parallelism and models. On production readiness, the recipes gain the operational tooling that long runs on Google Cloud expect: integration with the ML Goodput Measurement library, so a run reports how much of its chip time turns into preserved training progress and where the rest goes, and with Multi-Tier Checkpointing on GKE, which keeps checkpoints in local memory and in-cluster replicas with Cloud Storage as the durable tier, so large jobs save often and resume quickly. On precision, the float8 recipe that today ships for Llama 3 70B, built on torchao's Float8Linear and v7x's native fp8 matmuls, extends to the rest of the model set. On hardware, Google has announced its eighth-generation TPUs, TPU 8t for pre-training and TPU 8i for inference and reinforcement learning, and TorchTitan will support them as they become available on Google Cloud, with the same recipes carried forward. On parallelism, expert parallelism for the MoE kernels is the open item named earlier, and long-context training is next after it, now that million-token context windows are the norm for frontier models. On models, the targets are the next size up from Qwen3-235B: Qwen3.8-Max, a 2.4-trillion-parameter mixture of experts, and Kimi K3, 2.8 trillion parameters with Kimi Delta Attention in place of standard attention on most layers, which will exercise the kernel plug-in path as much as the scaling one.

TorchTitan on TPU is no longer an experiment. The same codebase and plain-PyTorch model definitions train Llama 3 405B at about 57% MFU on up to 1,024 chips, and run Qwen3 235B, a mixture-of-experts model, on up to 1,024 chips in a single slice. In a weak-scaling test with a fixed per-chip batch, Qwen3 235B kept 92% of its per-chip throughput from 64 to 8,192 chips. That shows the framework, compiler and collectives hold up at pod scale, even though a model of this size would not be trained at that scale. The pieces that make this possible are not buried in one recipe. SimpleFSDP, the Graph Trainer with scan and the graph splitter, host offload, the Pallas kernel library and the optimization playbook are building blocks, each behind a flag and each documented with the number it is worth. For models that TorchTitan does not ship, the Transformers modeling backend runs 500+ Hugging Face models through the same trainer with the first optimizations already applied, and for teams that want more than that, the onboarding skill turns porting a model into a repeatable procedure that an engineer or an agent can follow.

That leaves two ways to use it. The first is to adopt TorchTitan as the training framework: pick a recipe, launch it on a slice, and build on a configuration whose performance is already known. The second is to keep the training code you have and take what you need: the kernels are standard PyTorch custom ops, SimpleFSDP and the Graph Trainer are modules you can import, the compiler flags and the playbook apply to any TorchTPU workload, and every recipe spells out what it enables and why. Either way, the fastest way to find out what this does for your model is to run it.

#10262. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

#10257. Dark mode toggles: two states are enough

Exactly the kind of nerdy UI/UX discussion I love:

Lea’s pushing back on light/dark mode implementations that display three state options for visitors:

[…] a good two state control can actually express all three states — users just need to apply the override the first time it becomes relevant.

The first figure succinctly illustrates the point:

In short: No preference? Fall back to the system settings and provide a setting to override that if that’s needed/preferred. Then that preference is stored in localStorage for future sessions.

Something I sorta missed the first time I saw the image is that perhaps we don’t even need to display two states when we know what color scheme is active:

Personally, I’m convinced. Less UI that accomplishes the same as more UI is generally a good thing. Lea’s point goes way beyond that, getting into well-considered reasons that go beyond simplicity.

An interesting consideration is what happens when toggling back to a state. Like, if we start with a Light system preference, toggle it to Dark, then back to Light. Do we get a new override preference or go back to the system setting would be an undefined localStorage value? Maybe it doesn’t matter because theme switching isn’t exactly the primary user intent:

Remember, this control is entirely tangential to the actual user goal for visiting the website. Even if their intent were to pin light instead of reverting to System (light), this is something they would only notice once these diverge, i.e. the OS switches to dark. At that point, fixing it is a single click away. It’s such an easy fix, that there is no point in dwelling on it further.

To be clear, Lea is referring to persistent UI. The guidance is targeting toggles that are always visible to the visitor. That’s really, really common in a site’s header/navigation (my personal site included). If that’s the pattern, then it could distract from the visitor’s primary intent. And the more options we see, the more cognitive dissonance is introduced.

I think there’s an even more compelling argument in the thread:

All I ever wanted was for my OS to be dark, not every website I look at. It was a huge leap by the browser vendors to tie all web content to that system preference.Anyway, I think the light/dark preference should be in the browser, and what you propose seems perfectly in line with that.

Yes, please! Development for the preference, but no need to show an override when the setting is in the browser itself. Then a tri-state toggle might be more acceptable.

Speaking of which, Lea does offer two scenarios where three states make sense:

Update: Lea has published a follow-up that summarizes the whole discussion her first post stirred up. What’s left to say? It’s that this guidance is purely for persistent toggles, such as those in a main navigation. If that’s the case, then her two-state pattern is the way to go. But the best solution? Maybe no toggle at all and let the system default do the work.

We've got lots of ways to help you connect with the greatest front-end community in all the land.

Sign up to get front-end roundups, browser updates, and exclusive content sent to your inbox bi-weekly.

I thought Lea’s approach was very interesting, but I couldn’t get past the fact it feels too magical and a degree of control feels to be lost.

I took a different approach presenting the three options (light/dark/system) with only two buttons and some different behaviour: https://vale.rocks/micros/20260810-0330

I have to respectfully disagree with this approach.

First of all, a three-button segmented control does not take up enough screen real estate to justify removing it. More importantly, this design sacrifices a core UX paradigm for the sake of minimalism: always make it clear to the user what the current state is and what will happen next.

By hiding the “System” state inside a two-state toggle, you create a dynamic deadlock. If my OS is currently in Dark Mode and I want to re-enable the automatic system sync for tomorrow morning, I literally cannot select it because the toggle is already showing “Dark”. A good UI must always allow users to express an intent for the future, rather than just reacting to the present moment. Transparency and control will always beat “clever” hidden logic.

I also prefer the “explicit” 3-state approach.

Bramus eloquently explains why it might be better – https://www.bram.us/2026/08/18/the-case-for-tri-state-dark-mode-toggles/

There’s also a nice article by Bramus on a counterpoint to the two states argument. He argues in favour of the tri-state. https://www.bram.us/2026/08/18/the-case-for-tri-state-dark-mode-toggles/

After reading both, I’m leaning into the tri-state UX.

#10254. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10253. WordPress.com Student Plan

So happy to see WordPress.com offering a pricing tier aimed at students that gives them free hosting for a year:

WordPress.com offers the Student plan free, so cost is never a barrier to learning web skills. The goal is for students of any subject to create and share their thoughts and work online, and to own their online presence from the start.

I also think it’s way overdue. As someone who teaches beginning web development, I find that building a WordPress site makes for a great final project to demonstrate the myriad skills covered over the course of a semester — HTML, CSS, design, hosting, etc. Hosting those projects has always been a roadblock though.

I’ve tried several othered things over the years: Shared hosting plans (which sets me back financially), WPSandox.io (gone bye-bye though there are alternatives), WordPress Playground (saving and sharing work is complicated), sponsored hosting (it’s a hard sell on a semester-by-semester basis), and on and on.

A student plan with a year-long leash straight from the source is the perfect solution. You might think that WordPress.com is too point-and-click compared to a self-hosted WordPress.org site, but dot-com offers all the same developer affordances for directly touching code these days.

It’s worth noting that the deal is only good for .blog and .art domains. Not a deal-breaker whatsoever when it comes to educational purposes. You can always point it elsewhere down the road if you want to spend a few bucks.

I do not agree. I think it an accident waiting to happen. Read my blog post here:

https://www.usecue.com/blog/wordpress-com-student-plan/

I read your post (nicely written!) but didn’t exactly see an explanation for why a student plan is a disaster waiting to happen.

Your core concerns appear to be: (1) limited development capability, and (2) security.

(1) The student plan still allows learners to touch code as you can any self-hosted WordPress site, complete with developer tools and access. I’ve said it in this post, but I’ve taught the core tenants of web fundamentals for years. It’s not about learning WordPress, but using WordPress as a platform for demonstrating those fundamentals. And if learners are capable of touching the code — as they can — then it’s capable of demonstrating retained learning objectives.

(2) WordPress is only singled out by those concerned about security because it’s the largest and most adopted CMS, by far. Every platform (minus static generators, I suppose) carry the same risks. WordPress aggressively releases security updates and I think it’s great that WordPress.com handles the updating overhead for users.

And I’m less concerned about plugin vulnerabilities as a whole, because the final project for my course does not rely on them. The project is designed to assess core web fundamentals rather than extending a CMS.

But that’s my course, specifically. It’s totally valid if you are teaching for different learning outcomes that would validate your concerns.

Really like the intent here. Apply a style when someone navigates from one specific page to another. The idea being it’d make the sources for cross-document view transitions declarative in CSS rather than managing that stuff in JavaScript.

I think. Please correct me.

And what about that :nav-source pseudo (which may be renamed to :navigation-source)? Again, gotta wrap my head around it all. But if I am understanding right, that’s to match the specific element that triggers the transition. It’s the source of the navigation, be it a link, image, div, or whatever element. I didn’t see anything in the spec draft about a corresponding pseudo that matches the element on the other side of the transition — say, :target? — but perhaps there’s no clear use case for it.

Speaking of pseudos, the spec defines another one called :link-to() that applies styles to a linked element that targets a certain location. Straight from the spec:

…which I imagine matches an element in HTML like:

…which I think is a lot like styling links based on their targeted destination? Maybe it’s more a DX convenience when there’s already a declared location?

One thing I’m already not liking is that I doubt these features would work for a site like CSS-Tricks that has such a flat URL structure. Most of our URLs are a single level deep. So, if I want to match between a specific page (/about) and any article (/article-url) there’s no way to distinguish that route. But maybe that says something about the way URLs are designed around here and they should have more structure. That’s not a trivial change though.

My friend Lee Meyer suggests that perhaps it would be possible to append a a parameter like ?blog on URL to gain URL pattern matching superpowers.

Oh, and while I’m thinking of it, I could see a possible use case where you’d want to apply certain styles when navigating from one specific page to another. Like, maybe you’re navigating between the homepage and an About page and want to style elements on the destination page based on where you’re coming from. Playing off the earlier example from Bramus:

I dunno. Seems like there could be security issues with that?

And Preethi chimed in on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel with what I think is insightful feedback:

It would’ve been great if we had an at-rule for all data infrastructures, similar to @property for all property-value pairs, with configurations valid as per type. We could’ve used it instead of @color-profile, @position-try, and now @location.

So true when there’s so many new rules to learn these days. Anything that lowers the learning curve is certainly worth considering.

You’ll want to peruse the spec draft for yourself to get deeper into the nitty-gritty of things like navigating based on navigation “type” (e.g., back, forward, reload) and navigation “phases” (e.g., loading, ready, committed).

It’s a lot to take in. But so are view transitions as a whole.

#10248. Announcing New vLLM and SGLang Inference Engines on TPU

As part of our broader announcements for PyTorch today, we are excited to launch native PyTorch backends for vLLM (developed in partnership with Inferact) and SGLang (developed in partnership with RadixArk).

For years, TPUs have powered large-scale AI workloads with high performance and efficiency. With the rise of agentic and multimodal workloads, serving efficiently is more important than ever. Both engines already run on TPU through JAX-based backends, but we kept hearing from developers that they wanted to customize and debug their serving stack in PyTorch.

So we started in earnest on TorchTPU, which runs PyTorch natively on TPU. We then built new vLLM and SGLang engines on top of it.

Our goal is for the PyTorch community to bring models to TPU and make them fast using the tools developers are already familiar with. The engines come with optimized, production-ready serving features and custom Pallas kernels.

Serving at scale requires co-design across every layer of the technology stack, from silicon to compilers to orchestration. Today's launch of new inference engines is at the central part of an integrated, open TPU ecosystem.

TorchTPU makes the TPU a native PyTorch device. Tensors are managed as standard PyTorch tensors on a tpu device with no wrapper types, code runs eagerly or compiles via torch.compile using XLA, and collectives go through torch.distributed.

Because each device runs its own PyTorch process, both engines keep each framework’s worker model, scheduler, KV cache manager, API server and so forth, with TPU implementations underneath.

Most models use the standard upstream PyTorch model definitions in both engines, with TPU-specific optimizations in kernels and layer implementations. Custom kernels written in Pallas are called directly from PyTorch as custom operators inside the compiled graph. This platform-plugin design reuses as much of each framework as possible while letting TPU optimizations move at their own pace.

Both engines are built for the full range of production workloads.

Agentic workloads in particular demand several of these features simultaneously. Because agents repeatedly resend expanding multi-turn conversations, serving them efficiently requires finding the cached prefix instead of recomputing it. Both engines keep reusable prefixes in HBM and, with host offload turned on, automatically move older prefixes to host memory as a second tier.

Two of these features move the KV cache away from the device that computed it. Disaggregated serving runs prefill and decode on separate TPU hosts, so each request's KV cache has to travel from the prefill host to the decode host.Tiered KV offload moves the part of the KV cache that no longer fits in HBM to the host's DRAM and secondary storage with Google Cloud Managed Lustre, and brings it back when a later request reuses the prefix. Either way, the transfer has to overlap computation or it adds directly to latency.

Both engines use TPU Sync for this. It is an open-source C++ library for KV cache transfer and management on TPU. It reads the device buffers behind PyTorch tensors directly, so a transfer needs no extra staging copy on the host, and it runs transfers on its own threads outside Python while prefill and decode continue. vLLM-TorchTPU exposes it through vLLM's KV connector interface. SGLang-TorchTPU uses it as a disaggregation transfer backend and as the I/O backend for HiCache.

Both engines are already running at scale in production with preview customers. To provide transparent, reproducible proof of our performance, we benchmark in public through the community-standard SemiAnalysis InferenceX project.

Building on our initial submission of Qwen 3.5 on Ironwood TPU (TPU 7x), our engineering teams have further optimized the stack, bringing the lowest cost per million tokens overall to an industry leading 2.01 cents. Alongside these improvements, we have also published new state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance results to AgentX, the project's dedicated track for evaluating agentic long-context and multi-turn workloads. Ironwood TPU leads across most of the Pareto frontier. We look forward to continuously updating our public performance profiles as we bring more models and configurations to the leaderboard.

We did not build these backends in a vacuum. By embracing PyTorch and working directly with open-source project maintainers, we ensure these engines remain upstream-aligned and community-first:

This partnership is bringing SGLang's stellar team to build for TPU, delivering an engine that is already running in production at Meta and battle-tested at scale across demanding real-world scenarios.

Together, we integrated a high-performance Pallas megakernel for the 2.8T-parameter Kimi K3 model that runs the entire decode step as one kernel, serving more than 700 tokens per second to a single user on Ironwood TPU. Building on this work, Inferact recently led our first preliminary AgentX submission for Kimi K3 on the vLLM-TorchTPU stack. This initial milestone already demonstrates exceptional performance per TCO in ultra-high-interactivity workloads, and the rapid increase in the maturity of the vLLM and PyTorch ecosystem on TPU for doing new frontier OSS models bring-up, with follow-up submissions set to unlock even greater efficiency through larger scale prefill/decode disaggregation.

To foster continuous open-source contribution, vLLM is also launching a first-of-its-kind community contribution TPU capacity pool to provide free TPU compute to developers who actively contribute to the vLLM engine.

As the models get larger, we are evolving both engines to make large-scale TPU inference deployments performant and efficient. The major features for production-grade agentic workloads are already supported. More performance optimizations and advanced features are under way – open a Github issue to request collaboration on yours!

Across the two engines, most of the popular open model families are supported, including Qwen, DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi, GPT-OSS, Gemma and more. The team is investing in making more models available to run natively on TPUs, including Day 0 support for new open models. We also want to give the community the tools and TPU capacity to tune performance for their own workloads and configurations.

vLLM-JAX and SGLang-JAX remain important projects. Both will continue to be maintained, supported, and available for the community to use and adapt.

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

Fast, reliable A2UI, with swappable inference formats

Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

#10247. Resolved: CSS Class Prefix Selector

Just looking at what Bramus shared the other day:

First off, if you don’t follow Bramus, where have you been?! Jokes aside, his job is to be first-in-line on new features like this — especially as it pertains to Chrome — so it’s worth keeping tabs on his RSS and/or social.

It’s not a new proposal. Lea posted it back in 2024 and has advocated for it the whole while. As Bramus mentions in his post, what’s new is that the proposal was formally adopted and, as of three days ago, it has been added to the Selectors Level 5 spec draft. So, chances are that we’ll see it formally adopted at some point and implemented somewhere even sooner.

I really like the ergonomics of it. Existing substring selectors — class^="prefix" and class=*" prefix" — are verbose and defintely less readable than a simple .prefix-*.

And it’s not like [data-attribute] selectors that require not only an extra step touching HTML but still added verbosity.

But something makes me wince at the idea. I can’t quite put my finger on it. Perhaps it’s redundancy as in, we can already do this with existing selectors? Bramus cites performance issues with existing substring selectors as a primary reason we need this. But Brian Kardell’s reply resonates with me:

Yeah, I guess if we have a very specific case we can optimize it more than a general one, but I'm not seeing how this is a lot more specific than the version above which almost exists today. Feels like if we could optimize this we could optimize that. I'd be curious how it is optimized.

Then again, I do like how we extended color functions for brevity, like:

And it’s backwards-compatible, so no real harm if you continue to use the “old” way. It’s not like substring selectors don’t have other use cases and become totally obsolete. But maybe that’s it: this isn’t an “upgrade” of something we already have, but a new thing that isn’t progressive enhancement out of the gate. We’d have to @support it until it becomes a Baseline feature:

…which may or may not be a long wait. But we don’t know. And if ergonomics are the selling point, then we’re losing that in the wait.

Should also note that the wildcard doesn’t match other conditions or non-dashed cases:

Another worthy note is that the spec currently implies (but doesn’t explicitly state) that this has the same specificity as a class selector, (0,1,0). That’d make sense, as .prefix-* is really no different than writing .prefix-variation.

That said, I like how it might possibly look in a nested syntax:

…and Dave’s plea to support selecting web components:

Maybe I just convinced myself that I like it. Again, I dunno. Just take the added convenience and move on! Yada yada.

Sign up to get front-end roundups, browser updates, and exclusive content sent to your inbox bi-weekly.

Only supporting it as a dashed prefix is kind of weird. I do like it as replacing attribe-starts-with, since for that to work it needs to be the first class of potentially multiple.

color: rgb(10, 20, 20 / .25) is invalid. The new syntax has spaces between the RGB components.

Furthermore, rgb(10 20 20) is different from rgb(10% 20% 20%).

The first one, rgb(10 20 20), is equivalent to #0a1414 or rgb(3.92% 7.84% 7.84%), a very dark seagreen. That is because numbers with no % in RGB are in the [0, 255] interval (so the % equivalent is 10/255*100%, 20/255*100%…).

The second one, rgb(10% 20% 20%) is equivalent to #1a3333 or rgb(26 51 51), some kind of seagreen, visibly lighter than the first.

#10245. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#10243. WordPress PHP-Only Block Registration

You want to build a custom WordPress block. But you don’t want to learn React, manage a build pipeline, or deal with NPM packages.

Seven and half years after blocks arrived in Core, WordPress introduces a way to build blocks without any of these things. All you need is PHP.

A traditional WordPress block needs to be registered twice. Once in PHP, and once in JavaScript.

But WordPress 7.0 introduces a new and streamlined approach, allowing you to register a block using only PHP.

Let’s use this feature to build a Hello World Block:

The block is fully functional in the block editor, and fits right in with all the other blocks:

The key addition is the 'autoRegister' => true flag in the supports section. When set, WordPress automatically generates the required JavaScript for your block based on the PHP registration. This includes the client-side registration, and the editor preview.

Attributes let users customize the block’s appearance and behavior. In traditional block development you not only need to define the attributes, but also build out the corresponding controls in the editor interface.

With PHP-only registration, all that is needed is the attributes definition during block registration:

This code registers a greeting attribute as a string, with a default value. WordPress generates the corresponding input control in the block’s Settings sidebar.

At first sight, there is a lot to like about PHP-only registered blocks. For any WordPress developer, it feels like the good old times when programming was simpler.

You might be tempted to delay learning JavaScript block development indefinitely. But the PHP-only approach has important limitations worth understanding.

The editor displays the HTML as returned by the block’s render_callback PHP function. When the block is first displayed or when the user interacts with one of its controls, the editor component requests a new PHP render from a REST API endpoint.

While blocks rendered this way integrate seamlessly into the editor, they are not part of the single page JavaScript application that powers the entire editor experience.

First, you cannot add any controls within the block preview. This means you are limited to the auto-generated controls in the Settings sidebar.

The default interaction mode with blocks is the block preview itself. Imagine that you need a testimonial block. With a JavaScript rendered block, you would build out the testimonial design, and allow editing in place.

With a PHP-rendered block you can only use the sidebar. And even here you are limited, as currently there’s no support for image uploads or multiline text.

With JavaScript, you can allow editing in the block, as well as in the sidebar. Additionally you have access to all the controls that WordPress Core uses, and can even implement your own.

But without JavaScript, you’ll always be limited to the options WordPress provides based on the registered attributes of your PHP-only block.

Secondly you cannot attach any JavaScript to markup in the block preview. Imagine you want to develop a block that pulls five related posts, and which displays them in a slider. For that you would output the markup, and then pass a DOM node to the JavaScript library which then transforms the raw markup into the desired slider interface.

This reliance on finding and manipulating DOM elements is typical for traditional JavaScript development. But with PHP-only registered blocks in the block editor, the markup is fetched asynchronously and replaced on every re-render. This makes interacting with the DOM of the block preview unreliable or impossible.

While the front end render works fine with JavaScript libraries, the editor authoring experience will not work correctly. Even if you manage to attach any event listeners on first load, these will be disconnected the moment the preview re-renders.

These limitations are caused by the architecture of this feature, and they will not change in the future.

On the initial load of the block editor, WordPress loads the post data from the database into a client-side store managed by JavaScript. Any changes that you make in the editor update this data store on the client side. But the database isn’t updated until you save the post.

PHP-only registered blocks bypass this client-side store. When a block renders, it queries the database directly. But the database might contain stale data compared to what’s currently in the editor. Even worse, the PHP-rendered block isn’t notified of changes in the client side data, so it can’t refresh when data changes.

Let’s take a practical example: Imagine you are building a block that displays a header element with the post title. When the user changes the title in the editor, your block will still show the value from the database. You would need to save the post and reload the editor for the changed title to show up in the PHP-only block.

This makes PHP-only blocks unsuitable for any block that displays data that the user can change in the editor like title, content, excerpt, features images, or attached terms.

PHP-only registered blocks render through a REST API endpoint. So the same code renders the editor preview and the front end. But there is a critical difference: the global state.

On the front end, blocks render within The Loop, which sets key global variables like $post. Template tags like the_title() or the_content() rely on these globals to know which post is displayed.

But REST APIs are stateless, and don’t rely on global state. The endpoint that renders the block editor preview accepts a post ID parameter, but the editor component does not pass it through. This means that your render callback has no way to know which post is edited.

This limits the functions that you can use in the block editor preview. Template tags or functions like get_post_meta() need to know about the post context.

This is a significant architectural limitation as of WordPress 7.0. This could be addressed by passing the post ID to the endpoint, but there are no concrete plans to change this at the time of this writing.

WordPress 7.0 supports only three attribute types: strings, numbers, and booleans. These map to four basic editor controls: text inputs, number inputs, checkboxes, and a dropdown.

This screenshot shows a block that uses all available user interface elements:

The dropdown element is the only advanced control, but it has a significant limitation: it does not support keyed arrays. This makes it impossible to have a label that differs from the stored value.

Let’s take the example of a related posts block where users can select a category. You want to display the category names in the dropdown, but store the category IDs. This isn’t possible.

Instead, you must choose between displaying names or slugs, which both are user-editable, and store that value:

Using slugs not only doesn’t look good in the interface, but this implementation will also break when renaming a category. All existing blocks referencing the old slug will not be able to pull the related posts. IDs are stabler, and would only be invalid when the category is deleted.

Beyond dropdowns, essential controls — like image uploads, rich text editors, or date pickers — are absent. This might change in future releases, but again, there are no plans for it as of yet.

It’s easy to get discouraged looking at these limitations. It is true that PHP-only registered blocks are a poor choice for building new blocks from scratch.

But I consider them to still be very valuable because there is one use case where these limitations do not matter: migrating legacy PHP code into block themes. This WordPress 7.0 feature is a real game changer when it comes to developers adopting block themes, which is still a barrier of sorts for many theme authors.

In my experience, block themes are more performant, easier to maintain, and faster to build than legacy themes. Yet many developers are still relying on classic themes. And that is not by choice, but because of existing PHP-based features.

Until now, migrating these features came up against nearly insurmountable barriers. First, the need to learn JavaScript block development, and set up an entirely new development workflow with dependency management and build pipelines. Second, the time needed to rewrite all this code in JavaScript.

PHP-only registered blocks remove both these obstacles.

In 2022, I wanted to migrate a classic theme to a block theme.

The content area and the footer were straightforward to rebuild with blocks. But the header was more complex, especially with the more limited block building features of the time.

So, rather than spending time rebuilding the header, I took the existing PHP-header, and wrapped it in a server-side rendered block.

That said, we need to be realistic. This header block was far from perfect. The block preview was not responsive, dropdowns didn’t work in the editor, and you could not edit anything.

Did it matter? Not at all. The block rendered perfectly on the front end, and the editor preview was good enough. And because of this approach, I could migrate the theme in hours instead of days.

Before PHP-only registration, building such blocks still required a solid JavaScript proficiency and build tooling. But now any PHP developer can use this migration path using the skills they already have.

PHP-only registered blocks are ideal for converting:

The blocks you create do not need to be perfect in the editor. What counts is that they render correctly on the front end. By using existing PHP code, adaptations to block themes will be minimal.

Here are a few things I’ve learned along the way as I’ve been playing with blocks registered with PHP.

There might be cases in which you want to have a different block rendering depending on whether the block is displayed in the admin, or on the front end.

Using the is_admin() function for this use case does not work, as it does not evaluate to true when the REST API endpoint generates the markup for the block editor preview.

But there is another function that we can use: wp_is_rest_endpoint(). If it returns true, it means that WordPress is generating a REST API endpoint request. But this could be any endpoint rendering posts, so we need to ensure that we’re dealing with the Block Renderer endpoint.

This shows different text and styling depending on whether the block is rendered in the editor or on the front end:

We’ve seen that WordPress out of the box does not give you access to the ID of the edited post in PHP-only registered blocks. There is a workaround though.

WordPress registers blocks in the init hook. This hook also runs on every admin page. When you edit a post, the ID of the edited post is passed as a GET argument in the URL, for example: https://css-tricks.com/wp-admin/post.php?post=5\&action=edit

This means that at the moment of the block registration, we can retrieve this ID. To pass it to the block, we use an attribute. But we do not want WordPress to add an interface element, so we set the source of the attribute to local.

This only works when editing an existing post. When a new post is created, there is no post ID passed through the URL. WordPress will create one when the post is first saved, and update the URL.

But this is done through JavaScript without triggering a new page load from the server. Meaning that the PHP won’t have an opportunity to access the post ID until a full page reload is done.

So, yeah, not the greatest approach. But it’s good enough to unblock you until WordPress Core adds a proper implementation to pass post data to PHP-only registered blocks.

There are situations in which it is difficult to achieve a decent preview in the editor. In certain situations, it’s even impossible.

Think, for example, of a newsletter form provided as a snippet of HTML and JavaScript. Due to the limitations we’ve seen, the editor preview will always look broken.

In a situation like this, you can implement a placeholder in the editor. This is a strategy that WordPress Core uses as well, as we can see for the Post Content block:

Users do not expect an exact preview in every case. Choose the best compromise between the time needed to achieve a proper block editor preview and the expected UX gain.

You can use WordPress optimized stylesheet enqueuing, which only enqueues stylesheets on the front end for the blocks present on that specific page.

The register_block_type function offers two arguments:

To add a CSS stylesheet, register it using wp_register_style() Then use the handle during block registration:

WordPress auto-generates a .wp-block-{namespace}-{block-name} class and adds it to the wrapper container of your block as part of get_block_wrapper_attributes().

If you need to add additional classes or styles, you can pass these to get_block_wrapper_attributes() in the render callback function.

It’s the best practice to use this class as the common root class for writing targeted styles. I prefer to use the Block, Element, Modifier (BEM) approach for writing block styles. It prevents my styles from clashing with styles provided by WordPress Core or other code.

A common scenario is that you will have existing CSS, and restructuring this code and the markup using BEM would be too much work. In that case I recommend using a unique prefix for these legacy classes.

If you are dealing with a website that uses a front-end framework like Bootstrap, avoid enqueuing any framework stylesheets. You need to only migrate the CSS instructions that the block needs, applying unique prefixes as described above.

There are two ways for WordPress to integrate the post editor into the admin:

WordPress started with the first approach but quickly realized that it made styling the block editor very difficult. Without an iframe, any admin styles can interfere with that styles of the block editor, including your custom blocks.

In practice, this means that your blocks can look different in the editor than they do on the front end. For simplicity you want to use the same styles across both the front end and the editor preview with minimal adjustment. And the iframed post editor allows you to do that.

As of WordPress 7.0, the post editor is iframed if all blocks in the post are Version 3 or higher. WordPress 7.1 will enforce the iframe approach independently of the blocks.

So, to simplify building blocks and prepare for the next release, I think it’s best to ensure that all blocks on your sites use the Block API Version 3.

JavaScript support for PHP-only registered blocks is limited to the front end. To add a script, you can register it, and then pass the handle to the view_script during registration:

WordPress will only enqueue this script when the block is present on the current page.

PHP-only registered blocks can use the Block Supports API, which allows opt-in to core features. Depending on the feature, the block editor will expose additional interface elements to the user. It will also add attributes to the block to store the user’s choices.

There are features that will work independently of the theme. Others need to be enabled by the theme through its theme.json file.

Here is an example enabling color customization for the text and background color:

WordPress will take care of the outputting the corresponding CSS classes and inline styles using get_block_wrapper_attributes().

Allowing users to customize the appearance is a good demonstration, but not something that you are likely to often use. So, let’s have a look at three useful options for PHP-only registered blocks.

All register blocks appear in the inserter by default. But this does not make sense for every block. Imagine, for example, that you use a block to migrate a legacy PHP feature only used in a single template.

In this scenario, you could set inserter to false to hide the block from the inserter. Hidden blocks stay fully functional.

Setting multiple to false allows the block to only be inserted once into each post. An example is the core More block.

Once a non-multiple block is inserted, the block’s icon is disabled in the inserter to prevent inserting a second instance.

Setting align to true enables all available alignment options:

The text alignments like left, center, and right are always available. Wide and full-width alignment are only enabled if the theme supports it.

WordPress handles outputting the necessary classes for the block’s design to reflect the desired alignment.

If you want to selectively enable alignments, you can specify them. The available options are left, center, right, wide, and full.

With these tips you should be able to make the most out of PHP-only registered blocks, even with the limitations in WordPress 7.0.

Remember the opening question: Was it worth waiting seven-and-a-half years for this?

For building new, feature-rich blocks, the answer is no. You need JavaScript to deliver the kinds of interactive and native-feeling editing experiences that WordPress users expect. PHP-only block registration won’t replace JavaScript-powered blocks, and nor should it.

Because it’s not what this feature is for.

PHP-only registered blocks are the solution for thousands of WordPress sites stuck with classic themes because of the high learning curve and high cost of rebuilding with JavaScript.

You can now take shortcodes, widgets, and template parts and port them to the block editor with the PHP skills you already have. No JavaScript. No build pipeline. No code duplication.

And these blocks that you build do not need to be perfect. As long as you can insert them into block content, and they render correctly on the front end, that is all that is needed.

That is the killer use case for this feature. And for that, the wait was worth it.

But beyond this feature, PHP-only registered blocks signal an important shift: WordPress Core is finally prioritizing developer experience. Even as someone who builds JavaScript-powered blocks regularly, I’ll admit that the process involves too much boilerplate code, and too much coordination between block.json, the PHP code, and the JavaScript. Which is not to mention that time spent setting up and maintaining the build pipeline.

Anything that we can do to make this process easier, or avoid it entirely, is more than welcome.

So if you have projects with legacy PHP code preventing a migration to a block theme, then WordPress 7.0 has removed your biggest obstacle.

Migrate these legacy features to blocks and unlock everything modern WordPress has to offer.
