#10241. Bringing Visual Graph Debugging to Custom Edge Compilers with Model Explorer

Model Explorer goes beyond high-level model graphs. See how Arm plugs in custom compiler IRs, verifies NPU delegation side-by-side, and maps cycle-level profiling traces directly onto your execution graph.

Machine learning models run locally on a wide range of hardware, from low-power edge devices to gaming graphics and physical AI systems. Running models efficiently on these targets requires compilation steps such as quantization, graph lowering, operator fusion, and hardware delegation.

Arm used Google AI Edge Model Explorer to give developers visibility into these pipelines. With Model Explorer's hierarchical graph visualization and plugin architecture, Arm built several open-source adapters that make edge model transformations easier to inspect.

When deploying a model to edge hardware, a framework change or operator constraint can alter the exported execution graph. Engineers often need to answer three questions:

Answering these questions by reading raw flatbuffers or text logs takes time, especially for large language models (LLMs) and neural networks. Arm needed a visual tool to inspect Arm-specific intermediate representations and hardware profiling traces.

To address these deployment challenges, Arm developed custom adapters for Google AI Edge Model Explorer to support several model formats (i.e., TOSA, VGF, and PTE), establishing a comprehensive visual workflow for on-device optimization. Using these extensions, engineering teams can validate exported pipelines, verify accelerator delegation, and pinpoint cycle-level performance bottlenecks directly on the model graph:

Arm engineers used Model Explorer to validate a PyTorch-trained Neural Super Sampling (NSS) model exported through the ExecuTorch Arm backend as a VGF file for Vulkan ML Extensions. While inspecting the VGF graph, the team found unintended TRANSPOSE operators introduced by a change in the ExecuTorch Arm backend.

Model Explorer enables users to inspect individual nodes of the model graph, such as the rescale operator depicted below, which displays the operator's attributes and SPIR-V tensor connections. This functionality assisted the Arm team in identifying and resolving the regression without requiring manual analysis of raw log files.

To optimize vision models for accelerators like the Arm Ethos-U85 NPU, Arm engineers used Model Explorer's split-pane view to compare CPU and NPU-delegated graphs side-by-side for a Ultralytics YOLO model.

The split-pane view showed the structural difference: the CPU graph displays each individual layer, while the NPU graph groups the network into a single EthosUBackend block between quantize and dequantize nodes. For partially supported models in LiteRT or ExecuTorch, this view helps identify un-delegated operators and split subgraphs.

To find latency bottlenecks on the Ethos-U NPU, Arm ran their model on their Fixed Virtual Platform (FVP) simulator and used Model Explorer's node data plugin to overlay cycle-accurate execution traces onto the graph. Color-coded heatmaps showed that a single TRANSPOSE node was the main latency bottleneck.

You can use Google AI Edge Model Explorer along with Arm's open-source adapters today to inspect and optimize your own on-device models locally via PyPI or GitHub.

Special thanks to the contributors across the Arm and Google AI Edge teams for their work on these adapters and integrations:

Product manager: Charlie Stokes (ARM), Derek Bekebrede

Designers: Ami Kubota, Ioannis Politis (ARM), Kathleen Low, Philip Watson (ARM)

Senior Sponsors: Bala Gattu (ARM), Cormac Brick, Joe Alderson (ARM), Matthias Grundmann, Sharbani Roy (ARM)

#10240. MicroLighter: Syntax Highlighter

Here we go! Syntax highlighting for code blocks without the complicated markup, spans, classes, and bloated JavaScript, courtesy of Uncle Dave.

MicroLighter is pretty much straight-up CSS leaning on Custom Highlights, using the ::highlight() pseudo… which is Baseline this year!

It accomplishes exactly every we need around here at CSS-Tricks — themes, line numbers, multiple language support, semantic markup, etc. — but without the added dependencies we rely on.

Chris made a custom WordPress block years and years ago that plugs in Prism.js. There’s certainly nothing wrong with Prism. It’s robust, supports a crap-ton of languages, is also super lightweight, and plenty battled-tested. Either way, I’ll certainly be looking at integrating this into our WordPress block.

It’s not that there isn’t any JavaScript involved. It’s that less of it is needed. And it leans so hard into modern CSS that it brings things like light-dark() support and custom properties along for the ride, meaning rolling your own theme is totally doable.

Plus, it’s super modular in the sense that you can snag the things you want à la carte: specific themes, just the languages you need to support, line numbers, a web component.

Reminds me too of this clever little web font that brings syntax highlighting along for the ride. I’ve been happily using that on my personal site for a while without any fuss.

Update (August 31, 2026): I just finished adding MicroLighter to this site in place of Prism. I haven’t benchmarked performance in terms of page speed, etc., but I can say that the savings is pretty significant. Where Prism came in at 35 KB raw (9.3 KB gzipped), MicroLighter is at 13.9 KB raw (5.2 KB gzipped). Not bad at all considering there are WordPress-specific customizations that had to be baked in.

We've got lots of ways to help you connect with the greatest front-end community in all the land.

Sign up to get front-end roundups, browser updates, and exclusive content sent to your inbox bi-weekly.

Syntax highlighting for code blocks without the […] bloated JavaScript

This is an unreasonable claim, even for CSS-Tricks. Have you looked at how much weight Prism.js adds? Its scripting accounts for around 23,776 bytes of https://css-tricks.com/wp-content/themes/CSS-Tricks-19/js/min/global-concat.min.js, under 8KB gzipped. I’m actually not even certain that MicroLighter is smaller, once you include roughly the same set of languages; they’re pretty similar. If you use its touted lazy loading of grammars, it will certainly be slower.

There are, and have always been, good options that require no JavaScript; whereas this one fundamentally requires it (Custom Highlights are DOM-only, no serialisation defined or in my opinion likely to ever be defined).

Another limitation of this approach, which I reckon is important to mention, is that you are very limited in what you can style: simplifying slightly, it’s just color, background-color, text-decoration and text-shadow.

It’s very common to use font-weight: bold or font-style: italic in syntax highlighting. You can’t do that with this approach.

Well, like I said, it checks the boxes of what I’m looking for in a tool like this. It’s not that there aren’t alternatives that pack in much more features, again, like I said. I’m more interested in that MicroLighter doesn’t even touch the DOM.

#10237. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10236. animation-trigger

Experimental: Check browser support before using this in production.

The CSS animation-trigger property delays the start of a CSS animation until a specific trigger occurs. More specifically, it listens for a named trigger and controls how the animation plays or pauses in response.

This behavior has traditionally been JavaScript territory, typically with the Intersection Observer API.

Scroll-triggered animations should not be confused with scroll-driven animations. Although both rely on scroll or view timelines, they are fundamentally different concepts. We’ll get to those differences in a minute.

The animation-trigger property is defined in the Animation Triggers specification

The property accepts none, or a comma-separated list of triggers and their corresponding actions:

The “trigger” in animation-trigger can refer to either timeline-based triggers, such as scroll or view progress timelines, or event-based triggers like DOM events (e.g., a click). In this entry, we’ll mainly focus on timeline triggers. If you’re curious about event-based triggers, you can check out the official spec.

By default, trigger names have a global scope. If multiple elements define the same trigger name, the element that comes later in the cascade gets chosen. You can restrict the scope of a trigger to a specific DOM subtree with the trigger-scope property.

Some actions aren’t  or  only, meaning it’s possible to play-backwards when entering and play-forwards when exiting.

To actually use animation-trigger, you’ll typically need to set up a timeline trigger first. That controls when an animation starts based on where an element is within a timeline (like scroll or viewport position). More specifically, it activates when the element enters a defined activation range within that timeline.

To set up your timeline trigger, you’ll first define a custom  (like --fade-in) to link it to your animation-trigger , followed by a  timeline like a view() or scroll() function.

Then, you specify the  (such as contain) to dictate exactly when the trigger turns “on” inside the viewport.

You can also provide an optional  to define the outer boundary where the trigger stays active before turning “off”, though if you leave it out, the browser just defaults to your activation range.

To get a better feel for how this works, check out the timeline ranges visualizer from the Chrome team.

If you use different ranges, the active range must include the activation range; otherwise, the trigger can’t turn “on.”

While you can use all the individual longhand properties:

…you’ll almost always want to use the shorthand instead:

Unlike many CSS shorthands (like background or border), the order of values matters here, so you can’t rearrange them freely.

It’s worth noting that triggers and animations don’t have to be on the same element. You can define the timeline-trigger on a parent and apply the animation-trigger to multiple child elements. When the parent enters view, all children can animate together.

Let’s create a simple text reveal animation. We’ll use an element as the trigger point. Once you scroll past it, the animation plays and the text fades in.

First, define a timeline trigger on the trigger element:

This sets up a trigger named --trigger that activates based on scroll position. The range contain / cover means the animation is triggered when the element is fully visible in the scrollport (contain) and continues to run as long as any part of it remains visible in the scrollport (cover).

Next, we’ll apply the animation-trigger to the text you want to animate, along with the animation itself:

The cool part is that you can mix and match different animation-action values to get very different behaviors using the same trigger. Here’s a quick demo showing the same setup with different animation actions:

With scroll-driven animations, the animation’s progress is directly tied to scroll position. As you scroll, the animation scrubs forward or backward in sync with the timeline. There’s no concept of a “start” or “fire” moment.

In contrast, scroll-triggered animations are state-based rather than continuous. A trigger has a binary state and when a condition is met—such as an element entering a defined range—the trigger fires an associated action (like play, pause, or reset). Once triggered, the animation behaves like your regular CSS animations with no link to the scroll progress.

Here’s a pretty cool demo to see the differences between the two, courtesy of utlitybend:

The animation-trigger property is defined in the Animation Triggers specification, which is currently in Editor’s Drafts. That means the information can change between now and what it becomes an official Candidate Recommendation.

#10233. Creating Web Widgets Using the Document Picture-in-Picture API

Firefox 151 recently shipped the Document Picture-in-Picture API. This isn’t the same thing as the (regular?) Picture-in-Picture API, which pushes videos into a resizable window that remains visible even after switching browser tabs or OS windows. No, the Document Picture-in-Picture API enables us to put anything into the window.

I guess we can think of these windows as web widgets. We can use them for floating stock tickers, live chat conversations, playlists, to-do lists, notes, spreadsheets — anything that we’d want to keep on the screen at all times.

The general idea is that we create a Document Picture-in-Picture window (DPIP window), and then we put HTML, CSS, and JavaScript into it. It’s pretty simple when you think about it, but as we explore how the Document Picture-in-Picture API works, we’re going to tackle a slightly more complex scenario that you’ll probably run into.

We’re going to clone a stock ticker from the main document into a DPIP window. This not only gives us an opportunity to talk about some relevant media queries and pseudo-classes, which we’ll use to write targeted CSS for the DPIP window, but it’s also a stark reminder that taking a HTML component out of context can break the CSS, so you’ll need to keep that in mind.

But for it to work, you’ll need to open the demo in debug mode. This is because picture-in-picture doesn’t work in nested browsing contexts such as CodePen
